Decay estimates for discrete bi-Laplace operators with potentials on the lattice ℤ \mathbb{Z} Thanks: The work is partially supported by NSFC No.12171182 and the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities
Sisi Huang and Xiaohua Yao

Abstract
. It is known that the discrete Laplace operator Δ \Delta on the lattice ℤ \mathbb{Z} satisfies the following sharp time decay estimate: ‖ e i ​ t ​ Δ ‖ ℓ 1 → ℓ ∞ ≲ | t | − 1 3 , t ≠ 0 , \big\|e^{it\Delta}\big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0, which is slower than the usual O ⁡ ( | t | − 1 2 ) O(|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}) decay in the continuous case on ℝ \mathbb{R} . However, this paper shows that the discrete bi-Laplacian Δ 2 \Delta^{2} on ℤ \mathbb{Z} actually exhibits the same sharp decay estimate | t | − 1 4 |t|^{-\frac{1}{4}} as its continuous counterpart. In view of the free decay estimate, we further investigate the discrete bi-Schrödinger operators of the form H = Δ 2 + V H=\Delta^{2}+V on the lattice space ℓ 2 ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}) , where V V is a class of real-valued decaying potentials on ℤ \mathbb{Z} . First, we establish the limiting absorption principle for H H , and then derive the full asymptotic expansions of the resolvent of H H near the thresholds 0 0 and 16 16 , including resonance cases. In particular, we provide a complete characterizations of the different resonance types in ℓ 2 \ell^{2} -weighted spaces. Based on these results above, we establish the following sharp ℓ 1 − ℓ ∞ \ell^{1}-\ell^{\infty} decay estimates for all different resonances types of H H under suitable decay conditions on V V : ‖ e − i ​ t ​ H ​ P a ​ c ​ ( H ) ‖ ℓ 1 → ℓ ∞ ≲ | t | − 1 4 , t ≠ 0 , \big\|e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)\big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0, where P a ​ c ​ ( H ) P_{ac}(H) denotes the spectral projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum space of H H . Additionally, the decay estimates for the evolution flow of discrete beam equation are also derived: ‖ cos ⁡ ( t ​ H ) ​ P a ​ c ​ ( H ) ‖ ℓ 1 → ℓ ∞ + ‖ sin ⁡ ( t ​ H ) t ​ H ​ P a ​ c ​ ( H ) ‖ ℓ 1 → ℓ ∞ ≲ | t | − 1 3 , t ≠ 0 . \|{\cos}(t\sqrt{H})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}+\Big\|\frac{{\sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{ac}(H)\Big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0.



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Introduction

The analysis of time decay estimates for Schrödinger operators has long been a central theme in mathematical physics, originating from the fundamental studies of quantum dynamics in Euclidean space (see e.g.,
[
29
,
32
]
). Following these pioneering works, significant advances has been made across various dimensions and potentials (cf.
[
15
,
19
,
20
,
50
]
and references therein), with recent breakthroughs extending to higher-order Schrödinger operators (cf.
[
17
,
16
,
14
,
7
,
8
]
). These estimates, deeply connected to scattering theory and spectral analysis, have become indispensable tools in quantum dynamics and nonlinear analysis (cf.
[
53
,
54
,
49
,
41
]
).

A natural extension of this theory concerning discrete Schrödinger operators on the lattice space
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, has emerged as an equally important but mathematically distinct research area. A basic fact is that the discrete Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
exhibits the sharp decay estimates (cf.
[
51
]
)

\big\|e^{-it\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\big\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{d}{3}},\quad t\neq 0,

(1.1)

where
\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is defined by

(\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\phi)(n):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\phi(n+e_{j})+\phi(n-e_{j})-2\phi(n)),\quad\forall\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}),

(1.2)

with

e_{j}=(0,0,\cdots,0,\underset{j}{1},0,\cdots,0),\quad j=1,2\cdots,d.

Notably, this slower decay estimate (
1.1
) (compared with the
|t|^{-\frac{d}{2}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) reflects the distinct dispersion properties of lattice systems. Subsequent research has extended this result to perturbed operators on
\mathbb{Z}
(cf.
[
48
,
10
,
13
,
46
]
), yet the analysis of higher-order discrete Schrödinger operators remains largely unexplored.

Motivated by the purpose, in this paper, we are devoted to investigating the time decay estimates of the solutions for the following fourth order Schrödinger equation on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &i(\partial_{t}u)(t,n)-(\Delta^{2}u+Vu)(t,n)=0,\ \ (t,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z},\\
&u(0,n)=\varphi_{0}(n),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

and the discrete beam equation on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\partial_{tt}v)(t,n)+(\Delta^{2}v+Vv)(t,n)=0,\ \ (t,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z},\\
&v(0,n)=\varphi_{1}(n),\ \left(\partial_{t}v\right)(0,n)=\varphi_{2}(n),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

where
(\Delta\phi)(n):=\phi(n+1)+\phi(n-1)-2\phi(n)
for
\phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and
V
is a real-valued potential satisfying
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
with
\left<n\right>=(1+|n|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

The discrete bi-Laplace operator
\Delta^{2}
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
is the discrete analogue of the fourth-order differential operator
\frac{d^{4}}{dx^{4}}
on the real line. The equations (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) are discretizations of classical continuous models studied in
[
52
]
and
[
9
]
, respectively. These discretizations not only serve as numerical tools in computational mathematics but also hold profound significance in mathematics physics, particularly in quantum physics. For instance, discrete Schrödinger equations are standard
models for random media dynamics, as discussed in Aizenman-Warzel
[
1
]
, while the discrete beam equation describes the deformation of elastic beam under certain force (cf. Öchsner
[
47
]
).

Denote
H:=\Delta^{2}+V
. Then both
\Delta^{2}
and
H
are bounded self-adjoint operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, generating the associated unitary groups
e^{-it\Delta^{2}}
and
e^{-itH}
, respectively. The solutions to equations (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) are given as follows:

u(t,n)=e^{-itH}\varphi_{0}(n),

(1.5)

v(t,n)={\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H})\varphi_{1}(n)+\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}\varphi_{2}(n).

(1.6)

The expression (
1.6
) above depends on the branch chosen of
\sqrt{z}
with
\Im z\geq 0
, so the solution
v(t,n)
is well-defined even if
H
is not positive. In the sequel, we are devoted to establishing the time decay estimates of the propagator operators
e^{-itH}
,
{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H})
and
\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}
.

For the free case, i.e.,
V\equiv 0
, then
\sqrt{H}=-\Delta
. It follows from (
1.1
) that

\left\|e^{it\Delta}\right\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0.

(1.7)

As a consequence of (
1.7
), one has

\|{\rm cos}(t\Delta)\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}+\Big\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\Delta)}{t\Delta}\Big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

(1.8)

As stated before, the decay estimates (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) are slower than the usual
|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in the continuous case. However, interestingly, for the discrete bi-Laplacian on
\mathbb{Z}
, we can prove that

\big\|e^{-it\Delta^{2}}\big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},

(1.9)

which is sharp and the same as the continuous case on the line
[
52
]
, for details see Section
3
.

When
V\not\equiv 0
, the decay estimates for the solution operators of equation (
1.4
) are affected by the spectrum of
H
, which in turn depends on the conditions of potential
V
.
In this paper, we assume that the potential
V
has fast decay and
H
has no embedded positive eigenvalues in the continuous spectrum interval
(0,16)
. Under such assumptions, let
\lambda_{j}
be the discrete eigenvalues of
H
and
H\phi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}
for
\phi_{j}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
P_{ac}(H)
denote the spectral projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum of
H
and
P_{j}
be the projection on the eigenspace corresponding to the discrete eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
. Then the solutions of the equations (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) can be respectively further written as

u(t,n)=\sum\limits_{j}e^{-it\lambda_{j}}P_{j}\varphi_{0}(n)+e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)\varphi_{0}(n):=u_{d}(t,n)+u_{c}(t,n),

(1.10)

v(t,n)=v_{d}(t,n)+v_{c}(t,n),

(1.11)

where

\displaystyle v_{d}(t,n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j}{\rm cosh}(t\sqrt{-\lambda_{j}})\left<\varphi_{1},\phi_{j}\right>\phi_{j}(n)+\frac{{\rm sinh}(t\sqrt{-\lambda_{j}})}{\sqrt{-\lambda_{j}}}\left<\varphi_{2},\phi_{j}\right>\phi_{j}(n),

\displaystyle v_{c}(t,n)

\displaystyle={\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H})P_{ac}(H)\varphi_{1}(n)+\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{\sqrt{H}}P_{ac}(H)\varphi_{2}(n).

Observe that the discrete part
u_{d}(t,n)
of
u
has no any time decay estimates. Similarly, the existence of discrete negative/positive eigenvalues of
H
will lead to the exponential growth/dissipation of
v_{d}(t,n)
as
t
becomes large. Therefore, the main goal of this paper is to investigate the time decay estimates for the continuous components
u_{c}(t,n)
and
v_{c}(t,n)
in (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) under suitable decay conditions on
V
for all resonance types of
H
(see Definition
1.1
below).

To achieve this, our starting point is Stone’s formula. We first establish the limiting absorption principle for the operator
H
, and then study the asymptotic expansions of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda)
near thresholds
0
and
16
for all resonance types. Finally, we employ the Van der Corput Lemma to derive the desired estimates.

1.2.
Main results

In this subsection, we are devoted to illustrating our main results. To this end, we first give several notations and definitions. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
a\lesssim b
means
a\leq cb
with some constant
c>0
. Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, denote by
W_{\sigma}(\mathbb{Z})
:=
\underset{s>\sigma}{\bigcap}\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z})
the intersection space, where

\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z})=\Big\{\phi=\left\{\phi(n)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}:\|\phi\|^{2}_{\ell^{2,s}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<n\right>^{2s}|\phi(n)|^{2}<\infty\Big\}.

Note that
W_{\sigma_{2}}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq W_{\sigma_{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
if
\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{1}
and
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq W_{0}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
be defined on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
and
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
.

(I) Classification of resonances at threshold 0

(i)

0
is a regular point of
H
if the difference equation
H\phi=0
has only zero solution in
W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

0
is a first kind resonance of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but no nonzero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

0
is a second kind resonance of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but no nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iv)

0
is an eigenvalue of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(II) Classification of resonances at threshold 16

(i)

16
is a regular point of
H
if the difference equation
H\phi=16\phi
has only zero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

16
is a resonance of
H
if
H\phi=16\phi
has nonzero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but no nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

16
is an eigenvalue of
H
if
H\phi=16\phi
has nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Obviously, when
V\equiv 0
, both 0 and 16 are the resonances of
H
. This can be verified by taking
\phi_{1}(n)=cn+d
and
\phi_{2}(n)=(-1)^{n}c
with
c\neq 0
, which satisfy
\Delta^{2}\phi_{1}=0
and
\Delta^{2}\phi_{2}=16\phi_{2}
. Beyond this special case, there are some other non-trivial zero/sixteen resonance examples.

1. Example of resonance.
Consider the function
\phi(n)=2
for
n=0
and
\phi(n)=1
for
n\neq 0
and define the potentials
V_{1}(n)=-\frac{(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)}{\phi(n)}
and
V_{2}(n)=16+V_{1}(n)
. Then

(\Delta^{2}+V_{1})\phi=0,\quad(\Delta^{2}+V_{2})\phi=16\phi.

In this case, we have

V_{1}(n)=\left\{\begin{aligned} -3,&\quad n=0,\\
4,&\quad n=\pm 1,\\
-1,&\quad n=\pm 2,\\
0,&\quad{\rm else},\end{aligned}\right.,\quad V_{2}(n)=\left\{\begin{aligned} 13,&\quad n=0,\\
20,&\quad n=\pm 1,\\
15,&\quad n=\pm 2,\\
16,&\quad{\rm else}.\end{aligned}\right.

It indicates that
0
persists a resonance even for such compactly supported potential, and by shifting the potential by
16
one can turns the resonance at
0
into a resonance at
16
.

2. Example of eigenvalue.
Take
\phi(n)=(1+n^{2})^{-s}
with
s>\frac{1}{4}
,
V_{1}(n)=-\frac{(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)}{\phi(n)}
and
V_{2}(n)=16+V_{1}(n)
. At this time,

V_{1}(n)=O(\left<n\right>^{-4}),\quad|n|\rightarrow\infty.

This implies that
0
becomes an eigenvalue of
H
under such slowly decaying potential. However, as demonstrated later in Lemma
5.2
, the zero eigenvalue case is precluded for potentials with faster decay. Consequently, we do not necessarily discuss zero eigenvalue case in Theorem
1.2
.

Notably, the combinations of resonance types of
H
at these two thresholds exhibit considerable diversity for general potentials. This proves to be more intricate than both its continuous counterpart
[
52
]
and the second-order discrete Schrödinger operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
. In the continuous setting, we have only a single threshold
0
with the same resonance classification as described above. For the discrete Schrödinger operators
-\Delta+V
, both thresholds
0
and
4
are non-degenerate critical values. Furthermore, as established by Cuccagana and Tarulli in
[
10
]
, neither
0
nor
4
can be eigenvalues when the potential satisfies
\{\left<n\right>V(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The main results of this paper are summarized as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\mathcal{I}=(0,16)
, and let
P_{ac}(H)
denote the spectral projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum space of
H
. If

\displaystyle\beta>\left\{\begin{aligned} &15,\ 0\ is\ the\ regular\ point,\\
&19,\ 0\ is\ the\ first\ kind\ resonance,\\
&27,\ 0\ is\ the\ second\ kind\ resonance,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

then the following decay estimates hold:

\|e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,

(1.12)

and

\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}P_{ac}(H)\right\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0.

(1.13)

Remark 1.3
.

Some remarks on Theorem
1.2
are given as follows.

•

When
V\equiv 0
, the estimates (
1.12
) and (
1.13
) are sharp, as shown in Theorems
3.1
and
3.5
. Moreover, the decay rate remains unchanged in the presence of resonances.

•

Although two resonance types of
H
may coexist, we emphasize that the decay rate of the potential
V
above is fundamentally determined by the types of zero energy. The required rate
\beta
of
V
in Theorem
1.2
, derived from the asymptotical expansion of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
at
\mu=0
(see Theorem
1.8
below), might not be optimal. Hence, it would be interesting to investigate if there exists a minimal decay condition on
V
that satisfies the decay estimates above.

•

We note that the absence of positive eigenvalues has been an indispensable assumption in deriving all kinds of dispersive estimates. For
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
, Horishima and Lőrinczi have demonstrated in
[
22
]
that
H
has no eigenvalues in the interval
\mathcal{I}
for
V(n)=c\delta_{0}(n)

(c\neq 0)
, the
\delta
-potential with mass
c
concentrated on
n=0
. On the other hand, in contrast to the extensive results on the eigenvalue problems for discrete Schrödinger operators
-\Delta+V
, cf.
[
23
,
25
,
36
,
28
,
6
,
21
,
43
,
38
,
24
,
39
,
40
]
, more studies are needed to establish the absence of positive eigenvalue for higher order cases.

1.3.
The idea of proof

In this subsection, we outline the main ideas behind the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
Throughout this paper, we denote by
K
the operator with kernel
K(n,m)
, i.e.,

(Kf)(n):=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}K(n,m)f(m).

To derive Theorem
1.2
, based on the following two formulas:

{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H})=\frac{e^{-it\sqrt{H}}+e^{it\sqrt{H}}}{2},\quad\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H})}{t\sqrt{H}}=\frac{1}{2t}\int_{-t}^{t}{\rm cos}\left(s\sqrt{H}\right)ds,

it suffices to show that the estimates (
1.12
) and (
1.13
) hold for
e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)
and
e^{-it\sqrt{H}}P_{ac}(H)
, respectively. Using Stone’s formula, their kernels are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle(e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H))(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})-R^{-}_{V}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\big(e^{-it\sqrt{H}}P_{ac}(H)\big)(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{2}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})-R^{-}_{V}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(1.15)

Notice that the difference between (
1.14
) and (
1.15
) lies in the power of
\mu
in the exponent. This change affects the decay rate, which shifts from
\frac{1}{4}
to
\frac{1}{3}
.
In the following discussion, due to the similarity, we only address three fundamental problems that arise in the estimate of (
1.14
).

1.3.1.
Limiting absorption principle

According to (
1.14
), the first difficulty is to show the existence of boundary value
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
for any
\mu\in(0,2)
.

It is well-known that the limiting absorption principle (LAP) generally states that the resolvent may converge in a suitable way as
z
approaches spectrum points, which plays a fundamental role in spectral and scattering theory. For instance, see Agmon’s work
[
3
]
for the Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+V
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the discrete setting, the LAP for discrete Schrödinger operators
-\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has received much attention (cf.
[
12
,
56
,
4
,
5
,
33
,
34
,
27
,
42
,
48
]
and references therein).

However, to the best of our knowledge, it seems that LAP is open for higher-order Schrödinger operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Hence, based on the commutator estimates
[
30
]
and Mourre theory (cf.
[
2
,
44
,
45
]
), we will first demonstrate that under appropriate conditions on
V
,
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
for
H=\Delta^{2}+V
exist as bounded operators from
\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z})
for some
s
(see Theorem
2.4
).

1.3.2.
Asymptotic expansions of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})

As indicated in Theorem
2.4
, the second challenge lies in deriving the asymptotic behaviors of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
.

To this end, let
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
be the boundary value of the free resolvent
R_{0}(z):=(\Delta^{2}-z)^{-1}
, and define

M^{\pm}(\mu)=U+vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v,\quad\mu\in(0,2),\quad U={\rm sign}\left(V(n)\right),\quad v(n)=\sqrt{|V(n)|},

(1.16)

which is invertible on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
by the assumption of absence of positive eigenvalues in
\mathcal{I}
and Theorem
2.4
. Then

R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})=R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})-R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\left(M^{\pm}(\mu\right))^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4}),

(1.17)

from which we turn to study the asymptotic expansions of
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
.

The basic idea behind the expansions of
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}
is the Neumann expansion, which in turn depends on the expansion of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
. In this respect, Jensen and Kato initiated their seminal work in
[
29
]
for Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+V
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Since then, the method has been widely applied (cf.
[
31
,
52
]
).
When considering the discrete bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
, we will face two distinct difficulties.
Firstly, compared with Laplacian
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{Z}
, the threshold
0
now is a
degenerate critical value
(i.e.,
M(0)=M^{{}^{\prime}}(0)=M^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(0)=0
, where the symbol
M(x)=(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}
is defined in (
2.2
)). This degeneracy leads to additional steps to expand the
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}
. Secondly, in contrast to the continuous analogue
[
52
]
, we encounter another threshold 16 (i.e., corresponding to
\mu=2
).

More specifically, the kernels of boundary values
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
, as presented in (
2.8
), are given by

\displaystyle\ R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\left(\frac{\pm ie^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}-\frac{e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}\right),

where
\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfies
{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}
and
b(\mu)={\rm ln}\big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)
. This can be formally expanded near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
respectively as follows:

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-3}^{+\infty}\mu^{j}G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad(JR^{\pm}_{0}(2-\mu)^{4}J)(n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-1}^{+\infty}\mu^{\frac{j}{2}}\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

where
G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)
,
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)
are specific kernels defined in (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), respectively, and
J
is a unitary operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
given by

(J\phi)(n)=(-1)^{n}\phi(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(1.18)

Given these expansions of
R_{0}^{\pm}(\mu^{4})
above, the asymptotic expansions of
(M^{\pm}(\mu))^{-1}
can be derived near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
for all resonances types described in Definition
1.1
. Prior to giving these expansions, we introduce some involved spaces and operators. Let

\left<f,g\right>:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f(m)\overline{g(m)},\quad f,g\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

and

P:=\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\cdot,v\right>v,\quad\widetilde{P}:=JPJ^{-1}=\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\cdot,\tilde{v}\right>\tilde{v},

(1.19)

be the orthogonal projections onto the span of
v
,
\tilde{v}
in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, respectively, where
\tilde{v}=Jv
.
Denote

\displaystyle T_{0}=U+vG_{0}v,\quad\widetilde{T}_{0}=U+\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v},

(1.20)

where
G_{0}
and
\widetilde{G}_{0}
are integral operators with kernels defined in (
5.2
), (
5.4
), respectively.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
Q=I-P

(
where
I
is the identity operator
)
and
v_{k}(n)=n^{k}v(n),k\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
j\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, define
S_{j}
as the orthogonal projection onto the space
S_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, where

(i)

S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1\big\}=\left({\rm span}\{v,v_{1}\}\right)^{\bot}
,

(ii)

S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ S_{0}T_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(iii)

S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,2,\ QT_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(iv)

S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,2,3,\ T_{0}f=0\big\}
.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}=I-\widetilde{P}
,
\tilde{v}_{k}=Jv_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\widetilde{G}_{2}
be the integral operator with kernel defined in (
5.4
). For any
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, define
\widetilde{S}_{j}
as the orthogonal projection onto the space
\widetilde{S}_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, where

(i)

\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}\right>=0,\ \widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(ii)

\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ \widetilde{T}_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(iii)

\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ \widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}f=0\big\}
.

Remark 1.6
.

•

Notice that

Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\left({\rm span}\{v\}\right)^{\bot},\quad\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\left({\rm span}\{\tilde{v}\}\right)^{\bot},

and the spaces defined above have the following inclusion relations:

\displaystyle S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

•

From Lemma
5.2
later, we will further establish that
S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}=\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, which concludes the entire inversion procedure for
(M^{\pm}(\mu))^{-1}
. For more details, see Section
5
.

We point out that these projection spaces in Definitions
1.4
and
1.5
will be used to characterize the zero/sixteen resonance types of
H
, as detailed in Theorem
1.7
below.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>9
. Then we have

(i)

0
is a regular point of
H
if and only if
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(ii)

0
is a first kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
and
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(iii)

0
is a second kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
.

(iv)

16
is a regular point of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(v)

16
is a resonance of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
and
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(vi)

16
is an eigenvalue of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
.

With these characterizations, the expansions of
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}
can be obtained as follows.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Then for any
0<\mu_{0}\ll 1
, we have the following asymptotic expansions on

for

(i)

if

and

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=S_{0}A_{01}S_{0}+\mu QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q+\mu^{2}(QA^{\pm,0}_{21}Q+S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22}+A^{\pm,0}_{23}S_{0})+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,0}_{3}+\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu),

(1.21)

if

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}&=\mu^{-1}S_{1}A^{\pm}_{-1}S_{1}+\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q+QA^{\pm,1}_{02}S_{0}\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{11}+A^{\pm,1}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{\pm,1}_{13}Q\big)\\
&\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{\pm,1}_{21}+A^{\pm,1}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,1}_{3}+\Gamma^{1}_{4}(\mu),\end{split}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2}}{\mu^{3}}+\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0}+S_{0}A^{\pm}_{-2,2}S_{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q+QA^{\pm}_{-1,2}S_{2}+S_{0}A^{\pm}_{-1,3}S_{0}}{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}+A^{\pm,2}_{02}S_{2}+QA^{\pm,2}_{03}S_{0}+S_{0}A^{\pm,2}_{04}Q\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,2}_{11}+A^{\pm,2}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{\pm,2}_{13}Q\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{\pm,2}_{21}+A^{\pm,2}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,2}_{3}+\Gamma^{2}_{4}(\mu),

(1.23)

(iv)

if
16
is a regular point of
H
and
\beta>7
, then

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(2-\mu)\right)^{-1}=\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,0}_{1}+\Gamma^{0}_{1}(\mu),

(1.24)

(v)

if
16
is a resonance of

and

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(2-\mu)\right)^{-1}=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\big(\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01}+B^{\pm,1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\widetilde{Q}B^{\pm,1}_{03}\widetilde{Q}\big)+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,1}_{1}+\Gamma^{1}_{1}(\mu),

(1.25)

(vi)

if
16
is an eigenvalue of
H
and

, then

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(2-\mu)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}+\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1,1}\widetilde{Q}+\widetilde{Q}B^{\pm}_{-1,2}\widetilde{S}_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\big(\widetilde{Q}B^{\pm,2}_{01}+B^{\pm,2}_{02}\widetilde{Q}\big)+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,2}_{1}+\Gamma^{2}_{1}(\mu),

(1.26)

where

A_{01},A^{\pm}_{-1},A^{\pm}_{-3},B_{01},B^{\pm}_{-1},B_{-2},A^{\pm,i}_{jk},A^{\pm}_{j,k},A^{\pm,i}_{3},B^{\pm,i}_{jk},B^{\pm}_{j,k},B^{\pm,i}_{1}
are
\mu
-independent bounded operators on

defined in (
1.21
)
\sim
(
(vi)
) and
\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu)
are
\mu
-dependent operators satisfying the following estimates:

\|\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu)\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}+\mu\big\|\partial_{\mu}(\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu))\big\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}\lesssim\mu^{\ell}.

(1.27)

The detailed proofs of Theorem
1.8
and Theorem
1.7
will be presented in Section

and Section

, respectively.

1.3.3.
Treatment of oscillatory integral

Equipped with the two tools mentioned above, the final step is to handle the oscillatory integral (
1.14
) by Van der Corput Lemma
[
55
, P.
{332-334}
]
.
Specifically, we decompose (
1.14
) into three parts:

(e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H))=\frac{2}{\pi i}\Big(\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}+\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}+\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}\Big)e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}[R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})-R^{-}_{V}(\mu^{4})]d\mu,

(1.28)

where
\mu_{0}
is a sufficient small fixed positive constant.
Substituting (
1.17
) into the first and third integrals, and the following (
1.29
) into the second integral,

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})

\displaystyle=R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})-R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})+R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4}),

(1.29)

then we obtain

(e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H))(n,m)=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{3}(K^{+}_{j}-K^{-}_{j})(t,n,m),

(1.30)

where

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{0}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}R^{\mp}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\big(M^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{2}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\left[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})-R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\right](n,m)d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{3}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\big(M^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(1.31)

Thus, it suffices to show the decay estimate (
1.12
) holds for each component
K^{+}_{j}-K^{-}_{j}
, which will be dealt with in Section
3
and Section
4
.

1.4.
Organizations of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
provides some preliminaries, including the basics about free resolvent and the limiting absorption principle (Theorem
2.4
). In section
3
, we prove the decay estimate for the free case and demonstrate its sharpness. Section
4
focuses on estimating the kernels
(K^{+}_{j}-K^{-}_{j})(t,n,m)
defined in (
1.31
) for
j=1,2,3
.
Section
5
and Section
6
are devoted to presenting the proofs of Theorem
1.8
and Theorem
1.7
, respectively. Finally, we give a short review of commutators estimates and Mourre theory in Appendix
A
.

2.
Limiting absorption principle

2.1.
Free resolvent

This subsection provides some basics about the discrete bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
on
\mathbb{Z}
. Recalling the definition of
\Delta
on
\mathbb{Z}
in (
1.2
), the bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
on
\mathbb{Z}
is given by

(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)=(\Delta(\Delta\phi))(n)=\phi(n+2)-4\phi(n+1)+6\phi(n)-4\phi(n-1)+\phi(n-2).

Consider the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
:
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, defined by

(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(2\pi)^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{-inx}\phi(n),\quad\forall\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(2.1)

Under this Fourier transform, we have

(\mathcal{F}\Delta^{2}\phi)(x)=(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x):=M(x)(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}=[-\pi,\pi],

(2.2)

which implies that the spectrum of
\Delta^{2}
is purely absolutely continuous and equals
[0,16]
.
Let

R_{0}(z):=(\Delta^{2}-z)^{-1},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,16],

be the resolvent of
\Delta^{2}
and denote by
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)
its boundary value on
(0,16)
, namely,

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{0}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon),\quad\lambda\in(0,16).

Denote by
\mathbb{B}(s,s^{\prime})
the space of all bounded linear operators from
\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\ell^{2,s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z})
. The existence of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)
as an element of
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
follows from the resolvent decomposition

R_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{z}}\left(R_{-\Delta}(\sqrt{z})-R_{-\Delta}(-\sqrt{z})\right),\quad\sqrt{z}=\sqrt{|z|}e^{i\frac{argz}{2}},\ 0<argz<2\pi,

(2.3)

and the limiting absorption principle for
-\Delta
(cf.
[
33
]
)

R^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\mu):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{-\Delta}(\mu\pm i\varepsilon)\quad{\rm in\ the\ operator\ norm\ of}\ \mathbb{B}(s,-s)\ {\rm for}\ s>\frac{1}{2},\ \mu\in(0,4),

where
R_{-\Delta}(\omega)=(-\Delta-\omega)^{-1}
is the resolvent of
-\Delta
.

Furthermore, to derive the kernel of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)
, we recall the following fundamental fact for
-\Delta
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(
[
33
, Lemma 2.1]
)
For
\omega\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4]
, the kernel of resolvent
R_{-\Delta}(\omega)
is given by

R_{-\Delta}(\omega,n,m)=\frac{-ie^{-i\theta(\omega)|n-m|}}{2{\rm sin}\theta(\omega)},\quad n,m\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.4)

where
\theta(\omega)
is the solution of the equation

2-2{\rm cos}\theta=\omega

(2.5)

in the domain
\mathcal{D}:=\left\{\theta(\omega)=a+ib:-\pi\leq a\leq\pi,b<0\right\}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Precisely, let

C^{\pm}=\{\omega=x\pm iy:y>0\}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\mp}=\{\theta(\omega)=a+ib\in\mathcal{D}:\pm a<0\}
and define directed lines and line segments
\ell_{i},\ell^{\prime}_{i},\tilde{\ell}_{i}

\displaystyle\ell_{1}=\{x:x\in(-\infty,0)\},\quad\ell_{2}=\{x:x\in(0,4)\},\quad\ell_{3}=\{x:x\in(4,+\infty)\},

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{1}=\{ib:-\infty<b<0\},\quad\ell^{\prime}_{2}=\{a:a\in(0,\pi)\},\quad\tilde{\ell}_{2}=\{a:a\in(-\pi,0)\},

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{3}=\{\pi+ib:b\in(0,-\infty)\},\quad\tilde{\ell}_{3}=\{-\pi+ib:b\in(-\infty,0)\}.

Denote by
\ell^{-}_{i}
the line with opposite direction of
\ell_{i}
, then the map
\theta(\omega)
(defined in (
2.5
)) between
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4]\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}~(\omega\mapsto\theta(\omega))
has the following corresponding relation (see Figure
1
below).

x
y
0
4
C^{+}
C^{-}
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\ell_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\ell_{3}}
\omega

\theta(C^{\pm})=\mathcal{D}_{\mp}
\theta(\ell_{i})=\ell^{\prime}_{i}
\theta(\ell^{-}_{j})=\tilde{\ell}_{j}
(
i=1,2,3,\ j=2,3
)
{\rm\ \ cos}\theta(\omega)=1-\frac{\omega}{2}

\mathcal{D}_{+}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell^{\prime}_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\ell^{\prime}_{3}}
\theta

Figure 1.
Figure 1: The map
\theta(\omega)
from
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4]
to
\mathcal{D}
.

Consequently, from Lemma
2.1
, the following conclusions hold.

(i)

If
\lambda\in(0,4)
, the kernel of
R^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\lambda)
is given by

R^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\lambda,n,m)=\frac{-ie^{-i\theta_{\pm}(\lambda)|n-m|}}{2{\rm sin}\theta_{\pm}(\lambda)},

(2.6)

where
\theta_{\pm}(\lambda)
satisfies the equation
2-2{\rm cos}\theta=\lambda
with
\theta_{+}(\lambda)\in(-\pi,0)
and
\theta_{-}(\lambda)=-\theta_{+}(\lambda)
.

(ii)

If
\lambda\in(-\infty,0)
, then

{\rm sin}\theta(\lambda)=-i\sqrt{-\lambda+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}}=i\frac{e^{-i\theta(\lambda)}-e^{i\theta(\lambda)}}{2}.

(2.7)

Lemma 2.3
.

For
\mu\in(0,2)
, the kernel of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
is given by

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}\Big):=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m),

(2.8)

where
\theta_{+}:=\theta_{+}(\mu^{2})
satisfies
2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=\mu^{2}
with
\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
and

a_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}},\quad a_{2}(\mu)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}},\quad b(\mu)={\rm ln}\big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(2.9)

Proof.

For any
\mu\in(0,2)
and
\varepsilon>0
, let
z=\mu^{4}\pm i\varepsilon
in (
2.3
) and take limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, one obtains that

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=\frac{1}{2\mu^{2}}\left(R^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\mu^{2})-R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})\right).

(2.10)

Then based on (
2.4
), (
2.6
) and (
2.7
), the desired (
2.8
) is proved.
∎

As shown above,
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
exhibits singularity of order
\mu^{-3}
near
\mu=0
and
(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
near
\mu=2
. More precisely, they admit the following formal asymptotic expansions:

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-3}^{+\infty}\mu^{j}G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad(JR^{\pm}_{0}(2-\mu)^{4}J)(n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-1}^{+\infty}\mu^{\frac{j}{2}}\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

where
G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)
and
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)
are defined in (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), respectively. These expansions converge in the operator topology of suitable spaces
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
. For more details, see Lemma
5.4
.

2.2.
Perturbed case

As demonstrated in Subsection
2.1
, the limiting absorption principle (LAP) has been established for the free operator. In this subsection, we turn to the perturbed operators.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
and
\mathcal{I}=(0,16)
. Denote by
[\beta]
the biggest integer no more than
\beta
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

The point spectrum
\sigma_{p}(H)\cap\mathcal{I}
is discrete, each eigenvalue has a finite multiplicity and the singular continuous spectrum
\sigma_{sc}(H)=\varnothing
.

(ii)

Let
j\in\left\{0,\cdots,[\beta]-1\right\}
and
j+\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
, then the following norm limits

\frac{d^{j}}{d\lambda^{j}}(R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda))=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R^{(j)}_{V}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)\quad{\rm in}\quad\mathbb{B}(s,-s)

are norm continuous from
\mathcal{I}\setminus\sigma_{p}(H)
to
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
,

where
R_{V}(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
is the resolvent of
H
and
R^{(j)}_{V}(z)
denotes the jth derivative of
R_{V}(z)
.

We remark that the derivation of this LAP is based on the commutator estimates and Mourre theory (refer to Appendix
A
). The upper bound of
s
is closely related to the regularity of
H
(as defined in Definition
A.3
).

Throughout this paper, we always assume that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in
\mathcal{I}
. As a consequence of Theorem
2.4
,
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
exists in
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
with
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
for any
\mu\in(0,2)
. Moreover, as a corollary, we can establish the invertibility of
M^{\pm}(\mu)
(defined in (
1.16
)), which is critical to study the asymptotic behaviors of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
H,V,\mathcal{I}
be as in Theorem
2.4
. Then for any
\mu\in(0,2)
,
M^{\pm}(\mu)
is invertible on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and satisfies the relation below in
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
with
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{\beta}{2}
,

R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})=R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})-R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4}).

(2.11)

Proof.

For any
\mu\in(0,2)
, the invertibility of
M^{\pm}(\mu)
follows from the absence of eigenvalues in
\mathcal{I}
and Theorem
2.4
. Then the resolvent identity

R_{V}(z)=R_{0}(z)-R_{0}(z)v\left(U+vR_{0}(z)v\right)^{-1}vR_{0}(z),

(2.12)

combined with Theorem
2.4
and the fact
\left\{v(n)\left<n\right>^{-s}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{\infty}
for
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{\beta}{2}
gives the desired (
2.11
).
∎

In what follows, we are devoted to presenting the proof of Theroem
2.4
. To this end, it suffices to prove the following Proposition
2.6
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>1
and
\mathcal{I}=(0,16)
. Given
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\mathcal{J}
be the neighborhood of
\lambda
defined in (
2.15
) below. For any relatively compact interval
I\subseteq\mathcal{J}\setminus\sigma_{p}(H)
, define
\widetilde{I}=\{z:\Re z\in I,\ 0<|\Im z|\leq 1\}
. Then, for any
j\in\left\{0,\cdots,[\beta]-1\right\}
and
j+\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
, we have

(i)

\sup\limits_{z\in\widetilde{I}}\left\|R^{(j)}_{V}(z)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(s,-s)}<\infty.

(2.13)

(ii)

R^{(j)}_{V}(z)
is uniformly continuous on
\widetilde{I}
in the norm topology of
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
.

(iii)

For
\mu\in I
, the norm limits

\frac{d^{j}}{d\mu^{j}}(R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu))=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R^{(j)}_{V}(\mu\pm i\varepsilon)

exist in
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
and are uniformly norm continuous on
I
.

Before presenting the proof, we outline our main steps. Firstly, based on the theory developed by Jensen, Mourre and Perry in
[
30
]
(see also Theorem
A.2
), we aim to identify a suitable conjugate operator
A
. This operator will enable us to establish estimates for the derivatives of the resolvent
R_{V}(z)
in the space
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{s}
(the Besov space associated with
A
, as defined in
[
5
, Section 3.1]
). Subsequently, we will attempt to replace the space
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{s}
with
\ell^{2,s}
, thereby obtaining the desired results.

We now introduce the conjugate operator
A
considered here. Define the position operator
\mathcal{N}
:

(\mathcal{N}\phi)(n):=n\phi(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\forall\ \phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{N})=\Big\{\phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|n|^{2}|\phi(n)|^{2}<\infty\Big\},

and the difference operator
\mathcal{P}
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
:

(\mathcal{P}\phi)(n):=\phi(n+1)-\phi(n),\quad\forall\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

It immediately follows that the dual operator
\mathcal{P}^{*}
of
\mathcal{P}
is given by

(\mathcal{P}^{*}\phi)(n):=\phi(n-1)-\phi(n),\quad\forall\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

Let us consider the self-adjoint operator
A
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
satisfying

iA=\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{P}^{*}\mathcal{N}.

(2.14)

To apply Theorem
A.2
to our specific case, it suffices to verify two conditions: the regularity of
H
with respect to
A
and the Mourre estimate of the form (
A.2
). The first condition is verified in Lemma
A.6
, while for the second, we derive the following estimate.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
and let
A
be defined as in (
2.14
). Then, for any
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, there exist constants
\alpha>0
,
\delta>0
and a compact operator
K
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, such that

E_{H}(\mathcal{J})iad^{1}_{A}(H)E_{H}(\mathcal{J})\geq\alpha E_{H}(\mathcal{J})+K,\quad\mathcal{J}=(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta),

(2.15)

where
E_{H}(\mathcal{J})
represents the spectral projection of
H
onto the interval
\mathcal{J}
and
ad^{1}_{A}(H)
is defined in (
A.4
).

We delay the proof of this lemma to the end of this section. Now, combining this lemma and Lemma
A.6
, one can apply Theorem
A.2
to
H
to obtain the following estimates.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>1
and let
A
be defined as in (
2.14
). Given
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
is defined in (
2.15
). Then, for any relatively compact interval
I\subseteq\mathcal{J}\setminus\sigma_{p}(H)
, any
j\in\left\{0,\cdots,[\beta]-1\right\}
and
s>j+\frac{1}{2}
, one has

(i)

\sup\limits_{{\Re}z\in I,{\Im}z\neq 0}\left\|\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{(j)}_{V}(z)\left<A\right>^{-s}\right\|<\infty.

(2.16)

(i)

Denote
\widetilde{I}=\{z:\Re z\in I,\ 0<|\Im z|\leq 1\}
, then
\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{(j)}_{V}(z)\left<A\right>^{-s}
is Hölder continuous on
\widetilde{I}
with the exponent
\delta(s,j+1)
defined in (
A.3
).

(iii)

Let
\mu\in I
. The norm limits

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{(j)}_{V}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)\left<A\right>^{-s}

exist and equal

\frac{d^{j}}{d\mu^{j}}(\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu)\left<A\right>^{-s}),

where

\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu)\left<A\right>^{-s}:=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left<A\right>^{-s}R_{V}(\mu\pm i\varepsilon)\left<A\right>^{-s}.

The norm limits are Hölder continuous with exponent
\delta(s,j+1)
given by (
A.3
).

With Lemma
2.8
established, we proceed to prove Proposition
2.6
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

Given
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}
, any relatively compact interval
I\subseteq\mathcal{J}\setminus\sigma_{p}(H)
and
z\in\widetilde{I}
. Let
j\in\left\{0,\cdots,[\beta]-1\right\}
, differentiating both sides of the following resolvent identity
j
times

R_{V}(z)=R_{V}(-i)+(z+i)(R_{V}(-i))^{2}+(z+i)^{2}R_{V}(-i)R_{V}(z)R_{V}(-i)

yields

R^{(j)}_{V}(z)=(R_{V}(-i))^{(j)}+(z+i)^{(j)}(R_{V}(-i))^{2}+\sum\limits_{j_{1}+j_{2}=j}C^{j_{1}}_{j}((z+i)^{2})^{(j_{1})}R_{V}(-i)R^{(j_{2})}_{V}(z)R_{V}(-i).

\bm{(i)}
To obtain the (
2.13
), it suffices to show that for any
0\leq j_{2}\leq j
,

\sup\limits_{z\in\widetilde{I}}\left\|R_{V}(-i)R^{(j_{2})}_{V}(z)R_{V}(-i)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(s,-s)}<\infty,\quad j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta].

(2.17)

For this purpose, we decompose

\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-s}R_{V}(-i)R^{(j_{2})}_{V}(z)R_{V}(-i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-s}=\underbrace{\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-s}R_{V}(-i)\left<A\right>^{s}}\underbrace{\left<A\right>^{-s}R^{(j_{2})}_{V}(z)\left<A\right>^{-s}}\underbrace{\left<A\right>^{s}R_{V}(-i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-s}},

and establish the fact that

\left<A\right>^{s}R_{V}(\pm i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-s}\in\mathbb{B}(0,0),\quad 0\leq s\leq[\beta].

(2.18)

Then the desired (
2.17
) is obtained by combining the estimate (
2.16
).
To prove (
2.18
), obviously, it holds for
s=0
. Therefore, once we can show that

\left<A\right>^{[\beta]}R_{V}(\pm i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]}\in\mathbb{B}(0,0),

the desired (
2.18
) follows from complex interpolation. To see this, it further reduces to verify

A^{k}R_{V}(\pm i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]}\in\mathbb{B}(0,0),\quad\forall\ 1\leq k\leq[\beta],\quad k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}.

(2.19)

Indeed, using the formula

AR_{V}(\pm i)=-ad^{1}_{A}(R_{V}(\pm i))+R_{V}(\pm i)A=R_{V}(\pm i)\big(ad^{1}_{A}(H)R_{V}(\pm i)+A\big),

(2.20)

and by induction, we obtain that for any
1\leq k\leq[\beta]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{k}R_{V}(\pm i)&=R_{V}(\pm i)\big(ad^{1}_{A}(H)R_{V}(\pm i)+A\big)^{k}\\
&=R_{V}(\pm i)\sum\limits_{\ell_{j}\in\{0,1\},\ 1\leq j\leq k}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}\big(ad^{1}_{A}(H)R_{V}(\pm i)\big)^{\ell_{j}}A^{1-\ell_{j}}\Big).\end{split}

(2.21)

With the goal of combining the powers of
A
and
\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]}
, by utilizing the formula

Aad^{j}_{A}(H)=ad^{j}_{A}(H)A-ad^{j+1}_{A}(H),

and (
2.20
) repeatedly, one can express
A^{k}R_{V}(\pm i)\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]}
as a finite sum of operators with the form

T^{\pm}_{\ell}A^{\ell}\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]},\quad 0\leq\ell\leq k,

where
T^{\pm}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{B}(0,0)
, and if it contains term
ad^{q}_{A}(H)
, then
q
is at most
k
. Since
A^{\ell}\left<\mathcal{N}\right>^{-[\beta]}\in\mathbb{B}(0,0)
for any
0\leq\ell\leq k
in view of
k\leq[\beta]
, which gives the (
2.19
). Hence, the desired (i) is established.

\bm{(ii)}
and
\bm{(iii)}

can be followed by combining the uniform boundedness of (
2.17
) with (ii) and (iii) in Lemma
2.8
, respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we end this section with the proof of Lemma
2.7
.

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

For convenience, in this proof, we denote
H_{0}:=\Delta^{2}
and replace the notation
ad^{1}_{A}(\cdot)
with
[\cdot,A]
. For any
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}=(0,16)
, to obtain (
2.15
), the key step is to prove that it holds for
H_{0}
with
K=0
. Specifically, we need to show that there exist constants

\alpha>0
and
\delta>0
, such that

E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})[H_{0},iA]E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})\geq\alpha E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}),\quad\mathcal{J}=(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta).

(2.22)

Once (
2.22
) is established, after some deformation treatment, we have

\displaystyle E_{H}(\mathcal{J})[H,iA]E_{H}(\mathcal{J})=E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})[H_{0},iA]E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})+

\displaystyle\underbrace{E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})[H_{0},iA](E_{H}(\mathcal{J})-E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}))+(E_{H}(\mathcal{J})-E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}))[H_{0},iA]E_{H}(\mathcal{J})+E_{H}(\mathcal{J})[V,iA]E_{H}(\mathcal{J})}_{K_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq\alpha E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})+K_{1}=\alpha E_{H}(\mathcal{J})+\underbrace{\alpha(E_{H}(\mathcal{J})-E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}))+K_{1}}_{K},

where the compactness
K
follows from the fact that both
V
and
[V,iA]
are bounded compact operators under the assumption
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
with
\beta>1
. This establishes (
2.15
).

To establish (
2.22
), first by Lemma
A.6
,

[H_{0},iA]=2H_{0}(4-\sqrt{H_{0}}),\quad 0\leq H_{0}\leq 16.

Define
g(x)=2x(4-\sqrt{x})
for
x\in[0,16]
and let
\delta_{0}:=\delta_{0}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\min(\lambda,16-\lambda)
. Using functional calculus, we obtain

E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}_{1})[H_{0},iA]E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}_{1})\geq C(\lambda)E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}_{1}):=\alpha E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J}_{1}),\quad\mathcal{J}_{1}=[\lambda-\delta_{0},\lambda+\delta_{0}],

(2.23)

where
C(\lambda)=\min\limits_{x\in\mathcal{J}_{1}}g(x)
. Taking
\mathcal{J}=(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta)\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{1}
with
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, and multiplying both sides of (
2.23
) by
E_{H_{0}}(\mathcal{J})
, the desired inequality (
2.22
) is derived.
∎

3.
Decay estimates for the free case and sharpness

When
V\equiv 0
, the decay estimate (
1.13
) follows directly from the estimate
|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}
of
e^{it\Delta}
. Hence in this section, we will establish the decay estimate (
1.12
) for the free group
e^{-it\Delta^{2}}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

For
t\neq 0
, one has the following decay estimate:

\big\|e^{-it\Delta^{2}}\big\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(3.1)

Remark 3.2
.

It is well-known that decay estimate for
e^{it\Delta}
is derived using the Fourier
transform, whose kernel is given by

\left(e^{it\Delta}\right)(n,m)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-it(2-2{\rm cos}x)-i(n-m)x}dx.

Hence,

\|e^{it\Delta}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})}\lesssim\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-it\left(2-2{\rm cos}x-sx\right)}dx\Big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

Note that Fourier method may establish the decay estimate for
e^{-it\Delta^{2}}
.
However, we would like to use Stone’s formula to derive the free estimate (
3.1
) in Theorem
3.1
since it offers key insights for studying the perturbation case.
To this end, we first establish the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
t\neq 0
, the following estimate holds:

\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-it\left[(2\pm 2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-sx\right]}dx\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(3.2)

Proof.

This estimate is a concrete application of the Van der Corput Lemma (see e.g.
[
55
, P.
{332-334}
]
). Observing that by the change of variable
x\mapsto-\pi-y
, we obtain

\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-it[(2+2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-sx]}dx=e^{-its\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-it[(2-2{\rm cos}y)^{2}+sy]}dy.

Hence it suffices to prove that (
3.2
) holds for the “
-
” case.
For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\Phi_{s}(x):=(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-sx,\quad x\in[-\pi,0].

First, a direct calculation yields that

\Phi^{\prime}_{s}(x)=8(1-{\rm cos}x){\rm sin}x-s,\quad\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{s}(x)=8(1-{\rm cos}x)(2{\rm cos}x+1).

Note that
\Phi^{\prime}_{s}(0)=\Phi^{\prime}_{s}(-\pi)=-s
and

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{s}(x)=0,\ x\in[-\pi,0]\Longleftrightarrow x=-\frac{2\pi}{3}\ {\rm or}\ x=0,

it follows that
\Phi^{\prime}_{s}(x)
is monotonically increasing on
[-\frac{2\pi}{3},0]
and decreasing on
[-\pi,-\frac{2\pi}{3})
. Consequently, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the equation
\Phi^{\prime}_{s}(x)=0
has at most two solutions on
[-\pi,0]
. By the Van der Corput Lemma, slower decay rates of the oscillatory integral (
3.2
) for the “
-
” case occur in the cases of
s=0
and
s=-6\sqrt{3}
. For the other values of
s
, the rate is either
|t|^{-1}
or
|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

If
s=0
, then

\Phi^{\prime}_{0}(x)=0,\ x\in[-\pi,0]\Longleftrightarrow x=0\ {\rm or}\ x=-\pi.

Moreover, we can compute

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{0}(-\pi)=-16\neq 0\ {\rm and}\ \Phi^{\prime\prime}_{0}(0)=\Phi^{(3)}_{0}(0)=0,\Phi^{(4)}_{0}(0)=24\neq 0,

Thus, by the Van der Corput Lemma, (
3.2
) for the “
-
” case is controlled by
|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
.

If
s=-6\sqrt{3}
, then

\Phi^{\prime}_{-6\sqrt{3}}(x)=0,\ x\in[-\pi,0]\Longleftrightarrow x=-\frac{2\pi}{3},

and

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{-6\sqrt{3}}\left(-\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)=0\ {\rm but}\ \Phi^{(3)}_{-6\sqrt{3}}\left(-\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)\neq 0,

This implies that the decay rate is
|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}
. In summary, we derive the desired estimate of
|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
.
∎

From the proof above, we can immediately deduce the following corollary, which plays a key role in the estimates for the kernels
K^{\pm}_{j}(t,n,m)
defined in (
1.31
).

Corollary 3.4
.

(i)
For any interval
[a,b]\subseteq[-\pi,0]
, the estimate (
3.2
) still holds on
[a,b]
.

(ii)
Let
[a,b]\subseteq[-\pi,0]
. Suppose that
\phi(x)
is a continuously differentiable function on
(a,b)
with
\phi^{\prime}(x)\in L^{1}((a,b))
. Moreover,
\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow a^{+}}\phi(x)
and
\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow b^{-}}\phi(x)
exist. Then

\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{a}^{b}e^{-it\left[(2\pm 2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-sx\right]}\phi(x)dx\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\big|\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow b^{-}}\phi(x)\big|+\int_{a}^{b}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|dx\right).

(3.3)

Now we return to the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

First, using Stone’s formula, the kernel of
e^{-it\Delta^{2}}
is given by

\big(e^{-it\Delta^{2}}\big)(n,m)=\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4},n,m)d\mu=-\frac{2}{\pi i}(K^{+}_{0}-K^{-}_{0})(t,n,m),

where

K^{\pm}_{0}(t,n,m)=\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}R^{\mp}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)d\mu.

Then the desired estimate can be obtained by proving

|K^{\pm}_{0}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

From formula (
2.8
), we further express

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{0}(t,n,m)&=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\mp i\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}|n-m|}a_{1}(\mu)d\mu+\int_{0}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}a_{2}(\mu)d\mu\Big)\\
&:=\frac{1}{4}(K^{\pm}_{0,1}+K_{0,2})(t,n,m).\end{split}

(3.4)

For

{\bm{K^{\pm}_{0,1}(t,n,m)}}
, we consider the following variable substitution:

{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\theta_{+}}=\frac{{\rm sin}\theta_{+}}{\mu}=-\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}},

(3.5)

where
\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
and
\theta_{+}\rightarrow-\pi
as
\mu\rightarrow 2
.
Then
K^{\pm}_{0,1}(t,n,m)
can be rewritten as

K^{\pm}_{0,1}(t,n,m)=\mp i\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}\mp\theta_{+}\frac{|n-m|}{t}\big]}d\theta_{+},\quad t\neq 0.

Thus, it follows from Lemma
3.3
that

\sup\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}|K^{\pm}_{0,1}(t,n,m)|\leq\left|\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-it\left[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\right]}d\theta_{+}\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0.

(3.6)

For

{\bm{K_{0,2}(t,n,m)}}
, denote
F_{0}(\mu,n,m)=e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}a_{2}(\mu)
, using the Van der Corput Lemma directly, for
t\neq 0
, we have

|K_{0,2}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\Big(\big|\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 2^{-}}F_{0}(\mu,n,m)\big|+\big\|(\partial_{\mu}F_{0})(\mu,n,m)\big\|_{L^{1}((0,2))}\Big)\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},

(3.7)

where in the last inequality we used the following two key facts:

\big|\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 2^{-}}F_{0}(\mu,n,m)|=\big|\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2}}(3-2\sqrt{2})^{|n-m|}\big|\lesssim 1,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m,

and

b(\mu),b^{\prime}(\mu)<0\Longrightarrow\big\|\partial_{\mu}\big(e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}\big)\big\|_{L^{1}((0,2))}=\int_{0}^{2}-b^{\prime}(\mu)|n-m|e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}d\mu\leq 2.

(3.8)

Therefore, combining (
3.6
), (
3.7
) and (
3.4
), the desired estimate (
3.1
) is established.
∎

Finally, we demonstrate that the decay rate
\frac{1}{4}
in (
3.1
) is sharp, that is,
\frac{1}{4}
is the supremum of all
\alpha
for which there exists a constant
C_{\alpha}
such that

\left\|e^{-it\Delta^{2}}\phi\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq C_{\alpha}|t|^{-\alpha}\|\phi\|_{\ell^{1}},\quad t\neq 0,

(3.9)

holds for every sequence
\{\phi(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Consider the free discrete bi-Schrödinger inhomogeneous equation on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
:

i(\partial_{t}u)(t,n)-(\Delta^{2}u)(t,n)+F(t,n)=0,

with the initial value
\{u(0,n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then

(i)

The following Strichartz estimates hold:

\|\{u(t,n)\}\|_{L^{q}_{t}\ell^{r}}\leq C\big(\|\{u(0,n)\}\|_{\ell^{2}}+\|\{F(t,n)\}\|_{L^{\tilde{q}^{{}^{\prime}}}_{t}\ell^{\tilde{r}^{{}^{\prime}}}}\big),

(3.10)

where
(\tilde{q},\tilde{r}),(q,r)\in\big\{(x,y)\neq(2,\infty):\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{4y}\leq\frac{1}{8},\ x,y\geq 2\big\}
,
\tilde{q}^{{}^{\prime}},\tilde{r}^{{}^{\prime}}
denote the dual exponents of
\tilde{q}
and
\tilde{r}
, respectively and

\|\{u(t,n)\}\|_{L^{q}_{t}\ell^{r}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|u(t,n)|^{r}\Big)^{\frac{q}{r}}dt\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

(ii)

The decay estimate (
3.1
) is sharp.

Proof.

(i)
From the energy identity
\|\{u(t,n)\}\|_{\ell^{2}}=\|\{u(0,n)\}\|_{\ell^{2}}
and the decay estimate (
3.1
), the Strichartz estimates (
3.10
) follow directly from
[
37
, Theorem 1.2]
by Keel and Tao.

(ii)
To prove the sharpness of the decay estimate, we first establish the sharpness of the Strichartz estimates by constructing a Knapp counter-example. By duality, we have

\|\{e^{-it\Delta^{2}}u(0,n)\}\|_{L^{q}_{t}\ell^{r}}\leq C\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{\ell^{2}}\Leftrightarrow\left\|\phi(\cdot)\right\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq C\|\{F(t,n)\}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}\ell^{r^{\prime}}},

(3.11)

where

\phi(n)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it\Delta^{2}}F(t,n)dt.

Based on the Fourier transform defined in (
2.1
), one obtains that

\mathcal{F}\phi(x)=(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{f}_{time}(-(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2},x),

where
\hat{f}_{time}(s,x)
is the time Fourier transform of
f
, defined by

\hat{f}_{time}(s,x)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t,x)e^{-ist}dt,

and
f(t,x)=(\mathcal{F}F(t,\cdot))(x)
. Therefore, by Plancherel’s theorem, the right side inequality of (
3.11
) can be further expressed as follows:

\Big(\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\big|\hat{f}_{time}(-(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2},x)\big|^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C^{\prime}\|\{F(t,n)\}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}\ell^{r^{\prime}}}.

(3.12)

For any
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, we choose

\hat{f}_{time}(s,x)=\chi(\varepsilon^{-4}s)\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}x),

where
\chi
is the characteristic function of the interval
(-1,1)
. This yields that

F(t,n)=C^{\prime\prime}\frac{{\rm sin}\left(\varepsilon^{4}t\right)}{t}\frac{{\rm sin}(\varepsilon n)}{n}.

On one hand, using Taylor’s expansion
(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}=O(x^{4}),\ x\rightarrow 0,
we find that

\Big(\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\big|\hat{f}_{time}(-(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2},x)\big|^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\gtrsim\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

On the other hand, observe that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|{\rm sin}(\varepsilon n)|^{r^{\prime}}}{|n|^{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}+\sum\limits_{|n|>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\big)\frac{|{\rm sin}(\varepsilon(n))|^{r^{\prime}}}{|n|^{r^{\prime}}}\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\varepsilon^{r^{\prime}-1}+\sum\limits_{|n|>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|n|^{r^{\prime}}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{r^{\prime}-1},

then it follows that

\|\{F(t,n)\}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{t}\ell^{r^{\prime}}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{r}+\frac{4}{q}}.

Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary small, then
\frac{1}{2}\geq\frac{1}{r}+\frac{4}{q}
.

Then the decay rate in (
3.1
) is also sharp. Indeed, if not, i.e., there exists an estimate of the form (
3.1
) with
\alpha>\frac{1}{4}
. By
[
37
, Theorem 1.2]
, then this would imply Strichartz estimates in the range
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{\alpha}{r}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}
. Since
\alpha>\frac{1}{4}
, then there exists
q,r\geq 2
satisfying

\frac{1}{q}+\frac{\alpha}{r}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\ \ {\rm and}\ \ \frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{4r}>\frac{1}{8}.

This contradicts the sharpness of the Strichartz estimates established above.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

This section is devoted to presenting a detailed proof of (
1.12
) for
e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)
, from which (
1.13
) follows similarly.
To begin with, we recall the decomposition

(e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H))(n,m)=-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{3}(K^{+}_{j}-K^{-}_{j})(t,n,m),

(4.1)

where
K^{\pm}_{j}(t,n,m)(j=0,1,2,3)
are defined in (
1.31
).
As demonstrated in Section
3
, the estimate for
K^{\pm}_{0}(t,n,m)
has already been established. In what follows, we will focus on deriving the corresponding estimates for the kernels
(K^{+}_{j}-K^{-}_{j})(t,n,m)
with
j=1,2,3
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
and
K^{\pm}_{j}(t,n,m)(j=1,2,3)
be defined as in (
1.31
).

(i)

If

\displaystyle\beta>\left\{\begin{aligned} &15,\ 0\ is\ the\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&19,\ 0\ is\ the\ first\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&27,\ 0\ is\ the\ second\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.2)

then

\left|K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ \ uniformly\ in\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.3)

(ii)

If
\beta>2
, then

\left|K^{\pm}_{2}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ \ uniformly\ in\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.4)

(iii)

If

\displaystyle\beta>\left\{\begin{aligned} &7,\ 16\ is\ the\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&11,\ 0\ is\ the\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&15,\ 0\ is\ the\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.5)

then

\left|(K^{+}_{3}-K^{-}_{3})(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ \ uniformly\ in\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.6)

Once Theorem
4.1
is established, which together with Theorem
3.1
gives Theorem
1.2
. In the sequel, to obtain Theorem
4.1
, our proof will be divided into three subsections.

4.1.
The estimates of kernels
K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)

In this subsection, we establish the estimates (
4.3
) for kernels
K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)
under the assumptions (
4.2
). First, we give a crucial lemma, which plays a key role in eliminating the singularity of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
Q,S_{j}~(j=0,1,2)
be the operators defined in Definition
1.4
. Then for any
f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, the following statements hold:

(1)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQf)(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))\big({\bm{b_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{b_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho

\times v_{1}(m)(Qf)(m)
,

:=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(Qf)(m)
,

(2)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{j}f)(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)\big({\bm{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{c_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho\cdot v_{2}(m)

+{\bm{c_{3}(\mu)}}|n-m|v(m)\Big](S_{j}f)(m),

:=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(S_{j}f)(m),\quad j=0,1,

(3)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{2}f)(n)=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)^{2}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))^{3}\big({\bm{d_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{d_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho

\times v_{3}(m)+{\bm{d_{3}(\mu)}}|n-m|v(m)\Big](S_{2}f)(m)
,

:=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(S_{2}f)(m)
,

(4)

Q(vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f\big)=Qf^{\pm},\quad S_{j}\big(vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f\big)=S_{j}f^{\pm}_{j},\quad j=0,1,2
,

where
v_{k}(m)=m^{k}v(m)
,
a_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}
,
a_{2}(\mu)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}
and

•

b_{1}(\mu)=-\theta_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad b_{2}(\mu)=-b(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)
,

•

c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)=\mp i\theta^{2}_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad c_{2}(\mu)=(b(\mu))^{2}a_{2}(\mu),\quad c_{3}(\mu)=\theta_{+}a_{1}(\mu)+b(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)
,

•

d_{1}(\mu)=\theta^{3}_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad d_{2}(\mu)=-(b(\mu))^{3}a_{2}(\mu),\quad d_{3}(\mu)=2c_{3}(\mu),

•

f^{\pm}(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m),\quad f^{\pm}_{j}(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m),\quad j=0,1
,

•

f^{\pm}_{2}(n)=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m)
.

Remark 4.3
.

(1) Noting that
\theta_{+}
,
b(\mu)
and
c_{3}(\mu)
exhibit the following behaviors, respectively:

\theta_{+}=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad b(\mu)=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad c_{3}(\mu)=-\frac{2}{3}\mu^{3}+o(\mu^{3}),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}.

This indicates that, compared to the free resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-3})
, the operators considered in this lemma can decrease the singularity near
\mu=0
. Precisely, we have

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQ=O(\mu^{-2}),\quad R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{j}=O(\mu^{-1}),\quad R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{2}=O(1),\quad j=0,1,

\displaystyle QvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-2}),\quad S_{j}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-1}),\quad S_{2}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(1),\quad j=0,1.

(2) Additionally, recall that the kernel of free resolvent in the continuous analogue
[
52
]
is given by

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},x,y)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\big(\pm ie^{\pm i\mu|x-y|}-e^{-\mu|x-y|}\big),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

Observing that the counterpart
c_{3}(\mu)
in this circumstance vanishes. This difference implies that additional techniques are required to handle the decay estimates in our discrete setting.

The proof of this lemma will be delayed to the end of this subsection. Now, we prove the (
4.3
) case by case.

4.1.1.
Regular case

In this part, we present the proof of (
4.3
) for the case where
0
is a regular point of
H
. Recalling the kernels of
K^{\pm}_{1}

K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\big(M^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

and the expansions of
(M^{\pm}(\mu))^{-1}
in (
1.21
):

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=S_{0}A_{01}S_{0}+\mu QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q+\mu^{2}(QA^{\pm,0}_{21}Q+S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22}+A^{\pm,0}_{23}S_{0})+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,0}_{3}+\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu),

then
K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)
can be further decomposed as the finite sum

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)=\Big(\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}K^{\pm}_{A}+K^{\pm,0}_{r}\Big)(t,n,m),

(4.7)

where set
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\{S_{0}A_{01}S_{0},\mu QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q,\mu^{2}QA^{\pm,0}_{21}Q,\mu^{2}S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22},\mu^{2}A^{\pm,0}_{23}S_{0},\mu^{3}A^{\pm,0}_{3}\}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{A}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,\quad A\in\mathcal{A}_{0},\\
K^{\pm,0}_{r}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu)vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.\end{split}

(4.8)

Consequently, the estimate (
4.3
) reduces to establishing the corresponding estimates for
\{K^{\pm}_{A}\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}
and
K^{\pm,0}_{r}
. Next we will prove these estimates through three propositions.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>15
. Suppose that
0
is a regular point of
H
, then we have the following estimate:

\sup\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big|\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{0}A_{01}S_{0}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu\Big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0.

Proof.

Denote

K^{\pm}_{01}(t,n,m):=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{0}A_{01}S_{0}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

Applying Lemma
4.2
to
K^{\pm}_{01}(t,n,m)
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{01}(t,n,m)&=\frac{1}{16}\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\frac{1}{\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{0}A_{01}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu\\
&=\frac{1}{16}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{4}\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\frac{1}{\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}I^{\pm}_{j}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})(S_{0}A_{01}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu\\
&:=\frac{1}{16}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{4}K^{\pm,j}_{01}(t,n,m),\end{split}

(4.9)

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1}
,
M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2}
and
I^{\pm}_{j}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})
are given by

\displaystyle I^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{N_{1},M_{2}\}}\big(c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+c_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\big)d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times v_{2}(m_{1})v_{2}(m_{2}),

\displaystyle I^{\pm}_{2}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle=c_{3}(\mu)\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{1})\big(c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+c_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}\big)d\rho_{1}\cdot v_{2}(m_{1})|m-m_{2}|v(m_{2}),

\displaystyle I^{\pm}_{3}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle=c_{3}(\mu)\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{2})\big(c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}+c_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|M_{2}|}\big)d\rho_{2}\cdot|n-m_{1}|v(m_{1})v_{2}(m_{2}),

\displaystyle I^{\pm}_{4}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle=(c_{3}(\mu))^{2}|n-m_{1}|\cdot|m-m_{2}|v(m_{1})v(m_{2}).

(4.10)

By (
4.9
) and the symmetry between
I^{\pm}_{2}
and
I^{\pm}_{3}
, it suffices to establish the following decay estimates:

\sup\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\big|K^{\pm,j}_{01}(t,n,m)\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad j=1,2,4.

(4.11)

\bm{Case\ j=1.}

It follows from (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) that

K^{\pm,1}_{01}(t,n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})\Omega^{\pm}_{1}(t,N_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}(v_{2}S_{0}A_{01}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{1}(t,N_{1},M_{2})=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{N_{1},M_{2}\}}\Big(\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+\frac{c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\Big)d\mu:=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{4}\Omega^{\pm}_{1\ell}(t,N_{1},M_{2}),

with

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{11}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}\mu{\Big(\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)}^{2}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{12}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}e^{b(\mu)|M_{2}|}\mu\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{4}}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{13}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\Omega^{\pm}_{12}(t,M_{2},N_{1}),

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{14}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{b(\mu)(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}\mu{\Big(\frac{c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)}^{2}d\mu.

Next we show that for
1\leq\ell\leq 4
,

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{1\ell}(t,N_{1},M_{2})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},M_{2}.

(4.12)

Once this estimate holds, by Hölder’s inequality, it yields that

|K^{\pm,1}_{01}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.13)

(i)
For
\ell=1,2
, we perform the change of variable (
3.5
) to obtain

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{11}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{0}}^{0}e^{-it\left[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}\pm\theta_{+}\left(\frac{|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|}{t}\right)\right]}c_{11}(\mu(\theta_{+}))d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{12}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{0}}^{0}e^{-it\left[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}\pm\theta_{+}\frac{|N_{1}|}{t}\right]}c^{\pm}_{12}(\mu(\theta_{+}))e^{b(\mu(\theta_{+}))|M_{2}|}d\theta_{+},

where
r_{0}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfying
{\rm cos}r_{0}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}_{0}}{2}
and

c_{11}(\mu)=-\mu{\Big(\frac{\theta_{+}}{\mu}\Big)}^{4}a_{1}(\mu),\quad c^{\pm}_{12}(\mu)=\mp i\mu\frac{(\theta_{+}b(\mu))^{2}}{\mu^{4}}a_{2}(\mu).

Noticing that for
k=0,1
,

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\Big(\frac{\theta_{+}}{\mu}\Big)}^{(k)}\ {\rm and}\ \lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\Big(\frac{b(\mu)}{\mu}\Big)}^{(k)}\ {\rm exist},

this fact together with the estimate (
3.8
) and Corollary
3.4
gives that

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{1\ell}(t,N_{1},M_{2})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},M_{2},\quad\ell=1,2.

(4.14)

(ii)
For
\ell=4
, a direct application of Van der Corput Lemma and (
3.8
) give

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{14}(t,N_{1},M_{2})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},M_{2},

which together with (
4.14
) gives the (
4.12
) and thus the (
4.13
) is obtained.

\bm{Case\ j=2.}
By (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), we have

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{01}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{1})\Omega^{\pm}_{2}(t,N_{1})d\rho_{1}\cdot|m-m_{2}|v(m_{2})v_{2}(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{01}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2}),

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{\left<S_{0}f,v\right>=0}}\sum\limits_{m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(|m-m_{2}|-|m|)v(m_{2})\overline{S_{0}A^{*}_{01}S_{0}\overline{W^{\pm}}}(m_{2})

where
A^{*}_{01}
denotes the dual operator of
A_{01}
and

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{2}(t,N_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\big(c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+c_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}\big)d\mu,

\displaystyle W^{\pm}(t,n,m_{1})

\displaystyle=v_{2}(m_{1})\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{1})\Omega^{\pm}_{2}(t,N_{1})d\rho_{1}.

Since
\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\Big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu}\Big)}^{(k)}
exist for
k=0,1
, applying analogous arguments for
\Omega^{\pm}_{11}
and
\Omega^{\pm}_{14}
, we obtain

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{2}(t,N_{1})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1}.

Using Hölder’s inequality and triangle inequality, it yields that

|K^{\pm,2}_{01}(t,n,m)|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>v(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{2}}\|S_{0}A^{*}_{01}S_{0}\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}\|W^{\pm}\|_{\ell^{2}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.15)

\bm{Case\ j=4.}
As before, one has

\displaystyle K^{\pm,4}_{01}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu{\Big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)}^{2}d\mu\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(S_{0}A_{01}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2})|n-m_{1}|\cdot|m-m_{2}|v(m_{1})v(m_{2})

\displaystyle:=g(t)h(n,m).

Since
\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\Big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)}^{(k)}
exist for
k=0,1
, then using Van der Corput Lemma directly yields that
|g(t)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
. Furthermore, by virtue of
\left<S_{0}f,v\right>=0
and
S_{0}v=0
, we have

h(n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)v(m_{1})\sum\limits_{m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(S_{0}A_{01}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2})(|m-m_{2}|-|m|)v(m_{2}),

and consequently derive

|K^{\pm,4}_{01}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.16)

Hence, combining (
4.13
), (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), we establish (
4.11
), and thereby completing the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.4
, let
\mathcal{A}_{0}
be defined as in (
4.7
). Then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\setminus\{S_{0}A_{01}S_{0}\}
, we have the estimate

\sup\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big|\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu\Big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0.

(4.17)

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and symmetry, it suffices to handle the cases that
A=\mu QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q
,
\mu^{2}S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22},\mu^{3}A^{\pm,0}_{3}
. Let
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m):=16\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)
. By Lemma
4.2
and (
2.8
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-2}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu QA^{\pm,0}_{11}Q,\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22}v)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu^{2}S_{0}A^{\pm,0}_{22},\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(vA^{\pm,0}_{31}v)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu^{3}A^{\pm,0}_{31}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.18)

Substituting (
4.18
) into the integral in (
4.17
) and following the methodology of Proposition
4.4
, it is enough to estimate the following key oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,0}_{11}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{N_{1},M_{2}\}}\Big(\frac{b_{1}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+\frac{b_{2}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\Big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,0}_{22}(t,N_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu\Big(\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}+\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}\Big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,0}_{31}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2}\}}\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\Big)d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},\widetilde{N}_{1}=n-m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
. By virtue of the similar method as
\Omega^{\pm}_{1}
and
\Omega^{\pm}_{2}
in Proposition
4.4
, we can show that all these integrals are uniformly bounded by
|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
. This completes the proof of (
4.17
) for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\setminus\{S_{0}A_{01}S_{0}\}
.
∎

Therefore, this proposition combined with Proposition
4.4
gives the estimate (
4.3
) of
K^{\pm}_{A}
for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Then it remains to deal with the remainder
K^{\pm,0}_{r}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.4
, let
K^{\pm,0}_{r}(t,n,m)
be defined as in (
4.8
), then we have

\sup\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\big|K^{\pm,0}_{r}(t,n,m)\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0.

Proof.

From (
2.8
) and (
4.8
), we can express
K^{\pm,0}_{r}(t,n,m)
as

K^{\pm,0}_{r}(t,n,m)=\frac{1}{16}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{4}{\Omega}^{\pm}_{rj}(t,n,m),

where
N_{1}=n-m_{1}
,
M_{2}=m-m_{2}
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)=\frac{{\Gamma}^{0}_{4}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega^{\pm}_{r1}(\mu,n,m)&=-\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(a_{1}(\mu))^{2}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}(v\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)v)(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu,\\
\Omega^{\pm}_{r2}(\mu,n,m)&=\pm i\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}a_{1}(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}(v\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)v)(m_{1},m_{2})e^{b(\mu)|M_{2}|}d\mu,\\
\Omega^{\pm}_{r3}(\mu,n,m)&=\pm i\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}a_{1}(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}(v\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)v)(m_{1},m_{2})e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}d\mu,\\
\Omega^{\pm}_{r4}(\mu,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(a_{2}(\mu))^{2}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)v)(m_{1},m_{2})e^{b(\mu)(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}d\mu.\end{split}

By symmetry between
\Omega^{\pm}_{r2}
and
\Omega^{\pm}_{r3}
, it suffices to analyze the kernels
\Omega^{\pm}_{rj}(t,n,m)
for
j=1,2,4
.

On one hand, applying the variable substitution (
3.5
) to
\Omega^{\pm}_{rj}(t,n,m)
for
j=1,2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{r1}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=-\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-it(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}(v\varphi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+})v)(m_{1},m_{2})d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{r2}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\pm i\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-it(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}(v\varphi_{2}(\mu(\theta_{+})v)(m_{1},m_{2})e^{b(\mu(\theta_{+}))|M_{2}|}d\theta_{+},

where
\varphi_{j}(\mu)=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)a_{j}(\mu).

Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{r1}(t,n,m)\big|&\leq\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{r_{0}}^{0}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\big]}\Phi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))d\theta_{+}\Big|,\\
\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{r2}(t,n,m)\big|&\leq\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{r_{0}}^{0}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\big]}\Phi_{2}(\mu(\theta_{+}),m)d\theta_{+}\Big|,\end{split}

with
