\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(\mu)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})(\varphi_{1}(\mu)v)(m_{1}),\quad\Phi_{2}(\mu,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})\Big(\varphi_{2}(\mu)\big(v(\cdot)e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|}\big)\Big)(m_{1}).

In what follows, we show that

\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}\Phi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))=\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}\Phi_{2}(\mu(\theta_{+}),m)=0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ m\in\mathbb{Z},

(4.19)

and

\|\left(\Phi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))\right)^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}([r_{0},0))}+\left\|\frac{\partial\Phi_{2}}{\partial\theta_{+}}(\mu(\theta_{+}),m)\right\|_{L^{1}([r_{0},0))}\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.20)

Then, by Corollary
3.4
, we consequently obtain

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{rj}(t,n,m)\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z},\quad j=1,2.

(4.21)

Indeed, noting that by virtue of (
1.27
), for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]
,

\|\varphi_{j}(\mu)\|_{\mathbb{B}(0,0)}\lesssim\mu,\quad\sup\limits_{\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]}\left\|\varphi^{\prime}_{j}(\mu)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(0,0)}\lesssim 1,\quad j=1,2,

(4.22)

which combined with Hölder inequality implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\Phi_{1}(\mu)\right|+\left|\Phi_{2}(\mu,m)\right|\lesssim\mu,\quad\sup\limits_{\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]}\left|\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{1}(\mu)\right|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ m\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

(4.23)

This proves (
4.19
).
Since
\mu^{\prime}(\theta_{+})<0
, we have

\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\left|\left(\Phi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))\right)^{\prime}\right|d\theta_{+}=\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\left|\left(\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{1}\right)(\mu(\theta_{+}))\mu^{\prime}(\theta_{+})\right|d\theta_{+}\lesssim 1.

Moreover, one can calculate that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\big(\Phi_{2}(\mu,m)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})\big(\varphi^{\prime}_{2}(\mu)\big((v(\cdot)e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|})\big)\big)(m_{1})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})\big(\varphi_{2}(\mu)(v(\cdot)\partial_{\mu}(e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|}))\big)(m_{1}).

Then by (
4.22
), for any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\mu}\left(\Phi_{2}(\mu,m)\right)\right|\lesssim\|v\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}+\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}\left\|v(\cdot)\partial_{\mu}\left(e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|}\right)\right\|_{\ell^{1}},

which together with (
3.8
) gives that

\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\left|\frac{\partial\Phi_{2}}{\partial\theta_{+}}\left(\mu(\theta_{+}),m\right)\right|d\theta_{+}\lesssim 1+\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}|v(m_{1})|\int_{r_{0}}^{0}\left|\partial_{\mu}\left(e^{b(\mu(\theta_{+}))|m-m_{1}|}\right)\mu^{\prime}(\theta_{+})\right|d\theta_{+}\lesssim 1,

uniformly in
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, (
4.20
) is obtained.

On the other hand, the kernels of
{\Omega}^{\pm}_{r4}(t,n,m)
are as follows:

{\Omega}^{\pm}_{r4}(t,n,m)=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Phi_{4}(\mu,n,m)d\mu,

where

\Phi_{4}(\mu,n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})e^{b(\mu)|n-m_{1}|}\big(\varphi_{4}(\mu)\big(v(\cdot)e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|}\big)\big)(m_{1}),\quad\varphi_{4}(\mu)=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}(\mu)(a_{2}(\mu))^{2}.

Applying the Van der Corput Lemma directly, it follows that

\big|{\Omega}^{\pm}_{r4}(t,n,m)\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\big(|\Phi_{4}(\mu_{0},n,m)|+\big\|(\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{4})(\mu,n,m)\big\|_{L^{1}((0,\mu_{0}])}\big)\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,

(4.24)

uniformly in
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where the uniform boundedness of
|\Phi_{4}(\mu_{0},n,m)|
relies on the facts that
b(\mu)<0
and
\varphi_{4}(\mu)
also satisfies (
4.22
). The uniform estimate for the integral of
\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{4}
can be derived similarly to
\Phi_{2}(\mu,m)
. Therefore, combining (
4.21
) and (
4.24
), the desired result is obtained.
∎

In summary, through Propositions
4.4
\sim
4.6
, we establish the (
4.3
) for the regular case.

4.1.2.
First kind of resonance case

For this case, we first recall the expansion (
1.22
) that

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-1}S_{1}A^{\pm}_{-1}S_{1}+\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q+QA^{\pm,1}_{02}S_{0}\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{11}+A^{\pm,1}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{\pm,1}_{13}Q\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{\pm,1}_{21}+A^{\pm,1}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,1}_{3}+\Gamma^{1}_{4}(\mu),

then

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)=\Big(\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}K^{\pm}_{A}+K^{\pm,1}_{r}\Big)(t,n,m),

(4.25)

where

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{\mu^{-1}S_{1}A^{\pm}_{-1}S_{1},S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q,QA^{\pm,1}_{02}S_{0},\mu S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{11},\mu A^{\pm,1}_{12}S_{0},\mu QA^{\pm,1}_{13}Q,\mu^{2}QA^{\pm,1}_{21},\mu^{2}A^{\pm,1}_{22}Q,\mu^{3}A^{\pm,1}_{3}\}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{A}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,\quad A\in\mathcal{A}_{1},\\
K^{\pm,1}_{r}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{1}_{4}(\mu)vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.\end{split}

(4.26)

From Proposition
4.6
, it suffices to establish the estimate (
4.3
) for
\{K^{\pm}_{A}\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>19
. Suppose that
0
is a first kind resonance of
H
, then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, the following estimate hold:

|K^{\pm}_{A}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad uniformly\ in\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.27)

Proof.

Since the cancelation of
S_{1}
is same as
S_{0}
, consequently by removing the factor
\mu
, the proofs of Proposition
4.4
for
\mu^{-1}S_{1}A^{\pm}_{-1}S_{1}
and Proposition
4.5
for
\mu S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{11},\mu A^{\pm,1}_{12}S_{0}
remain valid. Moreover, Proposition
4.5
establishes the estimate (
4.27
) for
A=\mu QA^{\pm,1}_{13}Q,\mu^{3}A^{\pm,1}_{3}
. These observations combined with symmetry indicate that it suffices to show that (
4.27
) holds for
A=S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q,\mu^{2}QA^{\pm,1}_{21}
.

Denote
\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m):=16\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)
, then it follows from Lemma
4.2
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-3}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=S_{0}A^{\pm,1}_{01}Q,\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-1}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(QA^{\pm,1}_{21}v)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu^{2}QA^{\pm,1}_{21}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.28)

Taking (
4.28
) into the integral in (
4.27
), using the method of Proposition
4.4
, it reduces to estimate the following crucial oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{01}(t,N_{1},{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}+\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\frac{b_{1}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|{M}_{2}|}+\frac{b_{2}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{b(\mu)|{M}_{2}|}\Big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{21}(t,{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{b_{1}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|{N}_{1}|}+\frac{b_{2}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{b(\mu)|{N}_{1}|}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}\Big)d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
. Following the same method as used for
\Omega^{\pm}_{1}
and
\Omega^{\pm}_{2}
in Proposition
4.4
, then the desired (
4.27
) is obtained for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
∎

Therefore, this proposition together with Proposition
4.6
gives the proof of (
4.3
) for the first kind of resonance case.

4.1.3.
Second kind of resonance case

In this case, due to the high singularity, careful handling is required. First, as before, from the expansion (
1.25
)

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2}}{\mu^{3}}+\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0}+S_{0}A^{\pm}_{-2,2}S_{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q+QA^{\pm}_{-1,2}S_{2}+S_{0}A^{\pm}_{-1,3}S_{0}}{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}+A^{\pm,2}_{02}S_{2}+QA^{\pm,2}_{03}S_{0}+S_{0}A^{\pm,2}_{04}Q\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{\pm,2}_{11}+A^{\pm,2}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{\pm,2}_{13}Q\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{\pm,2}_{21}+A^{\pm,2}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}A^{\pm,2}_{3}+\Gamma^{2}_{4}(\mu),

(4.29)

we have

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{1}(t,n,m)=\Big(\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}K^{\pm}_{A}+K^{\pm,2}_{r}\Big)(t,n,m),

(4.30)

where
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is the set made up of all the terms with power of
\mu
no more than three occurred in (
4.29
) and

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{A}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,\quad A\in\mathcal{A}_{2},\\
K^{\pm,2}_{r}(t,n,m)&=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{2}_{4}(\mu)vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.\end{split}

Based on the preceding Propositions
4.6
and
4.7
, in this case, by also symmetry, we only need to prove the estimate (
4.3
) for
K^{\pm}_{A}
with
A=\mu^{-3}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2},\mu^{-2}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0}
,
\mu^{-1}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q,S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>27
. Suppose that
0
is a second kind resonance of
H
, then

|K^{\pm}_{A}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad uniformly\ in\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z},

(4.31)

for
A=\mu^{-3}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2},\mu^{-2}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0}
,
\mu^{-1}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q,S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}
.

Proof.

(i)
For
A=\mu^{-3}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2}
, we denote

K^{\pm}_{-3}(t,n,m):=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

From Lemma
4.2
, it can be further expressed as

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{-3}(t,n,m)=\frac{1}{64}\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu.

Following the method of Proposition
4.4
, the proof of (
4.31
) reduces to estimating three types of oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{-3}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{N_{1},M_{2}\}}\big(\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{-3}(t,N_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\big(\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}\big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,3}_{-3}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\Big)^{2}d\mu,\quad N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2}.

Since
\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}=\Big(\frac{\theta_{+}}{\mu}\Big)^{3}a_{1}(\mu)
and
\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}=-\Big(\frac{b(\mu)}{\mu}\Big)^{3}a_{2}(\mu)
, then for
k=0,1
,

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left(\frac{d_{j}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\right)^{(k)}\ {\rm exist},\quad j=1,2.

(4.32)

Using the method for
\Omega^{\pm}_{1}(t,N_{1},M_{2})
in Proposition
4.4
, we can immediately derive

\big|\Omega^{\pm,1}_{-3}(t,N_{1},M_{2})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},M_{2}.

(4.33)

For the remaining integrals containing
\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}
, we note that

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\quad{\rm exists\ but}\ \left(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\mu}+O(1)>0\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 0^{+}.

(4.34)

To establish the estimate (
4.33
) for
\Omega^{\pm,j}_{-3}
(
j=2,3
), we additionally verify the fact that

\displaystyle\|(d_{13}(\mu))^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}{((0,\mu_{0}])}}+\sup\limits_{N_{1}}\|\partial_{\mu}(d_{23}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|})\|_{L^{1}{((0,\mu_{0}])}}+\|(d_{33}(\mu))^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}{((0,\mu_{0}])}}\lesssim 1,

(4.35)

where

d_{13}(\mu)=\frac{d_{1}(\mu)c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{6}}
,
d_{23}(\mu)=\frac{d_{2}(\mu)c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{6}}
and
d_{33}(\mu)=\frac{(c_{3}(\mu))^{2}}{\mu^{6}}.

Indeed, for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]
, by (
4.32
) and (
4.34
), we have

|(d_{j3}(\mu))^{\prime}|\leq C+\big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\big)^{\prime},\quad|(d_{33}(\mu))^{\prime}|\lesssim\big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\big)^{\prime},\quad j=1,2.

The (
4.35
) then follows from (
3.8
) and the observation that

\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}\big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\big)^{\prime}d\mu=\frac{c_{3}(\mu_{0})}{\mu^{3}_{0}}+\frac{2}{3}<\infty.

This establishes estimate (
4.31
) for
A=\mu^{-3}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-3}S_{2}
.

(ii)
For
A=\mu^{-2}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0}
,
\mu^{-1}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q,S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}
, denote

\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m):=32\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m),

then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\pm}_{A}(\mu,n,m)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-5}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0})(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu^{-2}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-2,1}S_{0},\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-4}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=\mu^{-1}S_{2}A^{\pm}_{-1,1}Q,\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu^{-3}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m_{1})\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mu,m,m_{2})(S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}v)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ A=S_{2}A^{\pm,2}_{01}.\end{aligned}\right.

As before, the estimates reduce to analyzing the following oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{-2,1}(t,N_{1},{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}+\frac{2c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\frac{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|{M}_{2}|}+\frac{c_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{b(\mu)|{M}_{2}|}+\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}\Big)d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{-1,1}(t,{N}_{1},{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}+\frac{2c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\frac{b_{1}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|{M}_{2}|}+\frac{b_{2}(\mu)}{\mu}e^{b(\mu)|{M}_{2}|}\Big),

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{01}(t,{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\Big(\frac{d_{1}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}+\frac{d_{2}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|}+\frac{2c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu^{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}\Big)d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
. Applying the method for
\Omega^{\pm,j}_{-3}
, the desired estimate is derived.
∎

This proposition, together with Propositions
4.7
and
4.6
completes the proof of (
4.3
) for the second kind of resonance case. We thus conclude the full proof of Theorem
4.1
(i).

Finally, we end this subsection with complementing the proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Let
F^{\pm}_{1}(s)=e^{\pm is},F_{2}(s)=e^{-s}
, then equation (
2.8
) can be rewritten as

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\Big[\pm ia_{1}(\mu)F^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-m|)+a_{2}(\mu)F_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-m|)\Big].

(4.36)

(1)
Applying
[
52
, Lemma 2.5 (i)]
to
F^{\pm}_{1}
and
F_{2}
, and noting that

(F^{\pm}_{1})^{\prime}(s)=\pm iF^{\pm}_{1}(s),\quad F^{\prime}_{2}(s)=-F_{2}(s),

we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-m|)&=F^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n|)\pm i\theta_{+}m\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))F^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|)d\rho,\\
F_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-m|)&=F_{2}(-b(\mu)|n|)-b(\mu)m\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))F_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-\rho m|)d\rho.\end{split}

(4.37)

Substituting these two expressions into (
4.36
) and using the fact that
\left<Qf,v\right>=0
, we derive

\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQf\big)(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))\big(b_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+b_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho(v_{1}Qf)(m).

(2)
Define

\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(s)=F^{\pm}_{1}(s)\mp is,\quad\widetilde{F}_{2}(s)=F_{2}(s)+s,

(4.38)

from which a direct computation shows that
\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime}(0)=0=\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime}(0)
. By
[
52
, Lemma 2.5 (ii)]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-m|)&=\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n|)+\theta_{+}m({\rm sign}(n))\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime}(-\theta_{+}|n|)\\
&\quad+{(\theta_{+})}^{2}m^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho){\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)}^{\prime\prime}(-\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|)d\rho,\\
\widetilde{F}_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-m|)&=\widetilde{F}_{2}(-b(\mu)|n|)+b(\mu)m({\rm sign}(n))\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime}(-b(\mu)|n|)\\
&\quad+{(b(\mu))}^{2}m^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho){\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)}^{\prime\prime}(-b(\mu)|n-\rho m|)d\rho.\end{split}

(4.39)

For
j=0,1
, combining (
4.38
) and (
4.36
) yields

\displaystyle\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{j}f\big)(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[\pm ia_{1}(\mu)\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-m|)+a_{2}(\mu)\widetilde{F}_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-m|)

\displaystyle\quad+\big(\theta_{+}a_{1}(\mu)+b(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)\big)|n-m|\Big](vS_{j}f)(m).

Substituting (
4.39
) into this expression and using

\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime\prime}(s)=-{F}^{\pm}_{1}(s),\ \big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime\prime}(s)=F_{2}(s)\ {\rm and}\ \left<S_{j}f,v_{k}\right>=0,\quad k=0,1,

we obtain the desired (2).

(3)
Let

\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(s)=F^{\pm}_{1}(s)\mp is+\frac{1}{2}s^{2},\quad\widetilde{F}_{2}(s)=F_{2}(s)+s-\frac{1}{2}s^{2},

then
\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime}(0)=\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime\prime}(0)=0=\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime\prime}(0)=\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime}(0)
. Applying
[
52
, Lemma 2.5 (iii)]
, one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n-m|)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}(-\theta_{+}|n|)+\theta_{+}m({\rm sign}(n))\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime}(-\theta_{+}|n|)+\frac{{(\theta_{+})}^{2}m^{2}}{2}\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)^{\prime\prime}(-\theta_{+}|n|)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{{(\theta_{+})}^{3}m^{3}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{(1-\rho)}^{2}{({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))}^{3}{\big(\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{1}\big)}^{(3)}(-\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|)d\rho,

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{2}(-b(\mu)|n-m|)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{F}_{2}(-b(\mu)|n|)+b(\mu)m({\rm sign}(n))\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime}(-b(\mu)|n|)+\frac{{(b(\mu))}^{2}m^{2}}{2}\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)^{\prime\prime}(-b(\mu)|n|)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{{(b(\mu))}^{3}m^{3}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{(1-\rho)}^{2}{({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))}^{3}{\big(\widetilde{F}_{2}\big)}^{(3)}(-b(\mu)|n-\rho m|)d\rho.

Using the same method as in
(2)
and the property
\left<S_{2}f,v_{k}\right>=0
for
k=0,1,2
, part (3) follows.

(4)
For brevity, we only consider
QvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f
, as other cases are analogous.
By exchanging the roles of
n
and
m
in (
4.37
), substituting this new expression into (
4.36
) and using
Qv=0
, the desired result is obtained. This completes the whole proof.
∎

4.2.
The estimates of kernels
K^{\pm}_{2}(t,n,m)

This subsection is devoted to establishing the estimate (
4.4
) for
K^{\pm}_{2}
, thereby completes the proof of Theorem
4.1
(ii).
Recall from (
1.31
) that

K^{\pm}_{2}(t,n,m)=\left(K^{\pm}_{21}-K^{\pm}_{22}\right)(t,n,m),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{21}(t,n,m)&=\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big)(n,m)d\mu,\\
K^{\pm}_{22}(t,n,m)&=\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})VR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big)(n,m)d\mu.\end{split}

(4.40)

Proof of Theorem
4.1
(ii).

To derive (
4.4
), it suffices to establish

\displaystyle|K^{\pm}_{21}(t,n,m)|+|K^{\pm}_{22}(t,n,m)|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.41)

We begin by reformulating the free resolvent expression (
2.8
) as

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}\left(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)+a_{2}(\mu)e^{(b(\mu)\pm i\theta_{+})|n-m|}\right):=e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,n-m).

(4.42)

This form allows us to express the kernels (
4.40
) as

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{21}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{1}|)}\mu^{3}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,N_{1})\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,M_{1})d\mu\cdot V(m_{1})

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\Omega^{\pm}_{21}(t,N_{1},M_{1})V(m_{1}),

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{22}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu_{0}}^{2-\mu_{0}}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}\mu^{3}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,N_{1})(VR^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})V)(m_{1},m_{2})\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,M_{2})d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-m_{1},M_{1}=m-m_{1},M_{2}=m-m_{2}
.
Applying the variable substitution (
3.5
) to
\Omega^{\pm}_{21}
and
K^{\pm}_{22}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{21}(t,N_{1},M_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{0}}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}\pm\theta_{+}\big(\frac{|N_{1}|+|M_{1}|}{t}\big)\big]}F^{\pm}_{21}(\mu(\theta_{+}),N_{1},M_{1})d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{22}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{0}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}\pm\theta_{+}\big(\frac{|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|}{t}\big)\big]}F^{\pm}_{22}(\mu(\theta_{+}),n,m,m_{1},m_{2})d\theta_{+},

where
r_{1}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfying
{\rm{cos}}r_{1}=1-\frac{(2-\mu_{0})^{2}}{2}
,
\widetilde{a_{1}}(\mu)=\mu^{3}(a_{1}(\mu))^{-1}
and

\displaystyle F^{\pm}_{21}(\mu,N_{1},M_{1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a_{1}}(\mu)\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,N_{1})\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,M_{1}),

\displaystyle F^{\pm}_{22}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a_{1}}(\mu)\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,N_{1})(VR^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})V)(m_{1},m_{2})\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,M_{2}).

Noting that

\sup\limits_{\mu\in[\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}]}\big|\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,N)\big|+\big\|\big(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}\big)(\mu,N)\big\|_{L^{1}([\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}])}\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ N\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.43)

This estimate combined with Corollary
3.4
immediately yields

\big|\Omega^{\pm}_{21}(t,N_{1},M_{1})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},M_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.44)

For
K^{\pm}_{2}
, we denote

\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{22}(\mu,n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{\pm}_{22}(\mu,n,m,m_{1},m_{2}):=\widetilde{a_{1}}(\mu)\Psi^{\pm}(\mu,n,m).

In what follows, we will prove

\sup\limits_{\mu\in[\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}]}|\Psi^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)|+\|(\partial_{\mu}\Psi^{\pm})(\mu,n,m)\|_{L^{1}([\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}])}\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z},

(4.45)

which together with Corollary
3.4
gives

|K^{\pm}_{22}(t,n,m)|\leq\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{0}}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\big]}\widetilde{F}^{\pm}_{22}(\mu(\theta_{+}),n,m)d\theta_{+}\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(4.46)

Therefore, combining (
4.44
) and (
4.46
), the desired (
4.41
) is derived.

To verify (
4.45
),
first observe that for any
\mu\in[\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}]
, taking
\frac{1}{2}<\varepsilon_{1}<\beta-\frac{1}{2}
, the continuity of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
established in Theorem
2.4
and (
4.43
) yield

|\Psi^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)|\lesssim\left\|V(\cdot)\left<\cdot\right>^{\varepsilon_{1}}\right\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\left\|R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(\varepsilon_{1},-\varepsilon_{1})}\lesssim 1.

For the derivative term, through a direct calculation

\displaystyle(\partial_{\mu}\Psi^{\pm})(\mu,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=1}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\partial^{k_{1}}_{\mu}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}\big)(\mu,N_{1})(V\partial^{k_{2}}_{\mu}(R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4}))V)(m_{1},m_{2})\big(\partial^{k_{3}}_{\mu}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}\big)(\mu,M_{2}),

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=1}L^{\pm}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}(\mu,n,m),

and symmetry, it is enough to show that

\|L^{\pm}_{1,0,0}(\mu,n,m)\|_{L^{1}([\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}])}+\|L^{\pm}_{0,1,0}(\mu,n,m)\|_{L^{1}([\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}])}\lesssim 1,

(4.47)

uniformly in
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, for any
\mu\in[\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}]
, selecting
\frac{1}{2}<\varepsilon_{2}<\beta-1
and
\frac{3}{2}<\varepsilon_{3}<\beta-\frac{1}{2}
, respectively, we utilize Theorem
2.4
and (
4.43
) to obtain

\displaystyle\big|L^{\pm}_{1,0,0}(\mu,n,m)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\left<\cdot\right>^{\varepsilon_{2}}V(\cdot)\big(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}\big)(\mu,n-\cdot)\big\|_{\ell^{2}}\big\|R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(\varepsilon_{2},-\varepsilon_{2})}\big\|\left<\cdot\right>^{\varepsilon_{2}}V(\cdot)\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,m-\cdot)\big\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<m_{1}\right>^{\varepsilon_{2}}|V(m_{1})|\cdot\big|\big(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}\big)(\mu,n-m_{1})\big|,

\displaystyle\big|L^{\pm}_{0,1,0}(\mu,n,m)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\left<\cdot\right>^{\varepsilon_{3}}V(\cdot)\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,n-\cdot)\big\|_{\ell^{2}}\big\|\partial_{\mu}(R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4}))\big\|_{\mathbb{B}(\varepsilon_{3},-\varepsilon_{3})}\big\|\left<\cdot\right>^{\varepsilon_{3}}V(\cdot)\widetilde{R}^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,m-\cdot)\big\|_{\ell^{2}}\lesssim 1.

Applying (
4.43
) again, we get (
4.47
) and thereby complete the proof.
∎

4.3.
The estimate of kernel
(K^{+}_{3}-K^{-}_{3})(t,n,m)

In this subsection, we are dedicated to proving the estimate (
4.6
) for
(K^{+}_{3}-K^{-}_{3})(t,n,m)
under the assumptions (
4.5
).

4.3.1.
Regular case

First recall that the kernels of
K^{\pm}_{3}
in (
1.31
)

K^{\pm}_{3}(t,n,m)=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\big(M^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

and the asymptotic expansion (
1.24
) of
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}

\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}+(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,0}_{1}+\Gamma^{0}_{1}(2-\mu),\quad\mu\in[2-\mu_{0},2).

Then we further obtain

K^{\pm}_{3}(t,n,m)=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}K^{\pm}_{3j}(t,n,m),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\pm}_{31}(t,n,m)&=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big)(n,m)d\mu,\\
K^{\pm}_{32}(t,n,m)&=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vB^{\pm,0}_{1}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big)(n,m)d\mu,\\
K^{\pm}_{33}(t,n,m)&=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{0}_{1}(2-\mu)vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big)(n,m)d\mu.\end{split}

(4.48)

Thus, (
4.6
) can be obtained by establishing the decay estimates for each
K^{\pm}_{3j}(t,n,m)
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>7
. Assume that
16
is a regular point of
H
and let
K^{\pm}_{31}(t,n,m)
be defined as above. Then one has

\left|K^{\pm}_{31}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.49)

Prior to the proof, we emphasize that a key distinction from Subsection
4.1
lies that it is not straightforward to use the cancelation properties of
\widetilde{Q}
to eliminate the singularity near
\mu=2
. To overcome this, we employ the unitary operator
J
defined in (
1.18
), i.e.,
(J\phi)(n)=(-1)^{n}\phi(n)
, which satisfies

JR_{-\Delta}(z)J=-R_{-\Delta}(4-z),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4].

Through the limiting absorption principle, it yields that

JR^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\mu^{2})J=-R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2}),\quad\mu\in(0,2).

(4.50)

Combining this with the resolvent decomposition (
2.10
) and
J^{2}=I
, we obtain

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\mu^{4}}\Big[\Big(JR^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}+R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})v\widetilde{Q}\Big)B_{01}\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})J

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\Big(JR^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}+R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})v\widetilde{Q}\Big)B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})\Big].

(4.51)

This representation demonstrates that the singularity analysis of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
at
\mu=2
ultimately reduces to those of
R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}
and
\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})
..

Precisely, we have

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}
be as in Definition
1.5
and
\tilde{v}=Jv
. For any
f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then we have

(1)

\big(R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}f\big)(n)=(2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{-1}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}{\bm{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-\rho m|}d\rho\cdot\tilde{v}_{1}(m)(\widetilde{Q}f)(m)
,

:=(2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{-1}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\pm}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},n,m)(\widetilde{Q}f)(m)
,

(2)

\widetilde{Q}\big(\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})f\big)=\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{f}^{\pm},\quad\widetilde{f}^{\pm}(n)=(2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{-1}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\pm}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},m,n)f(m)
,
where
\tilde{\theta}_{+}
satisfies
{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1
with
\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
.

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

It follows from (
2.6
) that

R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2},n,m)=\pm i(2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{-1}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-m|}:=\pm i(2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{-1}F^{\mp}(-\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-m|),

(4.52)

where
\tilde{\theta}_{+}
satisfies
{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1
with
\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
and
F^{\mp}(s)=e^{\mp is}
.
Using the properties
\big<\widetilde{Q}f,\tilde{v}\big>=0
and
\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}=0
, and then following the proofs of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQf
and
Q(vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f\big)
in Lemma
4.2
, the desired result is obtained.
∎

Noting that
\tilde{\theta}_{+}=O((2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}})
as
\mu\rightarrow 2
, this lemma shows that these operators can eliminate the singularity of
R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})
.

With this lemma, now we proceed with the proof of Proposition
4.9
.

Proof of Proposition
4.9
.

From (
4.3.1
), the kernels
K^{\pm}_{31}(t,n,m)
can be further expressed as

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{31}(t,n,m)=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{4}K^{\pm,j}_{31}(t,n,m),

where

\displaystyle K^{\pm,j}_{31}(t,n,m)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{-1}\big(\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{31}(\mu)\big)(n,m)d\mu,

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda^{\pm,1}_{31}(\mu)&=JR^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})J,\\
\Lambda^{\pm,2}_{31}(\mu)&=R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})J,\\
\Lambda^{\pm,3}_{31}(\mu)&=JR^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2}),\\
\Lambda^{\pm,4}_{31}(\mu)&=R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}vR_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2}).\end{split}

(4.53)

By symmetry between
\Lambda^{\pm,2}_{31}
and
\Lambda^{\pm,3}_{31}
, it suffices to analyze the cases
K^{\pm,j}_{31}(t,n,m)
for
j=1,2,4
.

First, recalling from (
2.8
) that the kernel of
R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})
is given by

R_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2},n,m)=-\frac{1}{2\mu}a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}=\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mu,n,m).

(4.54)

This combined Lemma
4.10
gives that

\displaystyle\big(\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{31}(\mu)\big)(n,m)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{4{\rm sin}^{2}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\pm}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},n,m_{1})\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\pm}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},m,m_{2})(\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q})(m_{1},m_{2}),\ j=1,\\
&\frac{(-1)^{m}}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mu,n,m_{1})\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\pm}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},m,m_{2})(v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q})(m_{1},m_{2}),\ j=2,\\
&\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mu,n,m_{1})\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mu,m,m_{2})(v\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}v)(m_{1},m_{2}),\ j=4.\end{aligned}\right.

Furthermore, for any
j\in\{1,2,4\}
, the estimate for
K^{\pm,j}_{31}
ultimately reduces to that of
\Omega^{\pm,j}_{31}
, where

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{31}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}\mu^{-1}\Big(\frac{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\Big)^{2}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{31}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|M_{2}|}\mu^{-1}\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)\frac{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{N}_{1}|}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,4}_{31}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{-1}(\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu))^{2}e^{b(\mu)(|\widetilde{N}_{1}|+|\widetilde{M}_{2}|)}d\mu,

with
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},\widetilde{N}_{1}=n-m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
.

Obviously, (
4.49
) holds for
\Omega^{\pm,4}_{31}
by applying Van der Corput Lemma and (
3.8
) directly. As for
j=1,2
, we consider the change of variable

\displaystyle{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\tilde{\theta}_{+}}=-\frac{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{\mu},\quad\tilde{\theta}_{+}\rightarrow 0\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 2,

(4.55)

obtaining that

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{31}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{2}}^{0}e^{-it(2+2{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{2}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}F_{31}(\tilde{\theta}_{+})d\tilde{\theta}_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{31}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{2}}^{0}e^{-it(2+2{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+})^{2}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|M_{2}|}\tilde{\theta}_{+}\widetilde{F}_{31}(\mu(\tilde{\theta}_{+}),\widetilde{N}_{1})d\tilde{\theta}_{+},

where
r_{2}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfying
{\rm cos}r_{2}=\frac{(2-\mu_{0})^{2}}{2}-1
and

F_{31}(\tilde{\theta}_{+})=\frac{-\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{+}}{(2+2{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}){\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}},\quad\widetilde{F}_{31}(\mu,\widetilde{N}_{1})=-\frac{\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)}{\mu^{2}}e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{N}_{1}|}.

Since
\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow 0}\Big(\frac{x}{{\rm sin}x}\Big)^{(k)}
exist for
k=0,1
, this together with (
3.8
) and Corollary
3.4
yields that

\big|\Omega^{\pm,1}_{31}(t,N_{1},M_{2})\big|+\big|\Omega^{\pm,2}_{31}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ N_{1},\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

We note that both variable substitution (
3.5
) and (
4.55
) play important roles in handling the oscillatory integrals. However, they exhibit slight differences in addressing singularity. Specifically, the (
3.5
) does not alter the singularity near
\mu=0
, whereas (
4.55
) decreases a singularity of order
(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

This observation enables us to verify that
K^{\pm}_{32}
also likewise satisfy the decay estimates (
4.49
).

Proposition 4.12
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.9
, let
K^{\pm}_{32}(t,n,m)
be defined as in (
4.48
). Then

\left|K^{\pm}_{32}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.56)

Proof.

From (
4.48
) and (
2.8
), we have the representation

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{32}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{-3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}}(vB^{\pm,0}_{1}v)(m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\{N_{1},M_{2}\}}\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|\alpha|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|\alpha|}\Big)d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-m_{1},M_{2}=m-m_{2}
,
\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfying
{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}
and

a_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}},\quad a_{2}(\mu)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}.

The estimate (
4.56
) consequently reduces to proving corresponding estimates for these three types:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{32}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}\mu^{-3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}(a_{1}(\mu))^{2}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{32}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}\mu^{-3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{1}(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|M_{2}|}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,3}_{32}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{-3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}(a_{2}(\mu))^{2}e^{b(\mu)(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}d\mu.

For
\Omega^{\pm,3}_{32}
, it follows immediately from a direct application of the Van der Corput Lemma combined with (
3.8
). For the cases
j=1,2
, we employ the change of variable

{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\theta_{+}}=\frac{{\rm sin}\theta_{+}}{\mu}=-\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}=-(a_{1}(\mu))^{-1},

obtaining

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{32}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{r_{3}}e^{-it(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}F_{32}(\theta_{+})d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{32}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{r_{3}}e^{-it(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|N_{1}|}\widetilde{F}_{32}(\mu(\theta_{+}),M_{2})d\theta_{+},

where
r_{3}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfying
{\rm cos}r_{3}=1-\frac{(2-\mu_{0})^{2}}{2}
and

F_{32}(\mu)=2(2+\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-3},\quad\widetilde{F}_{32}(\mu,M_{2})=\mu^{-3}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|M_{2}|}.

Then it can be easily verified concluded from Corollary
3.4
that the estimates hold for
\Omega^{\pm,j}_{32}
for
j=1,2
.
∎

Finally, the estimates for
K^{\pm}_{33}(t,n,m)
can be derived by a similar argument of Proposition
4.6
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Under the assumptions in Propositions
4.9
, let
K^{\pm}_{33}(t,n,m)
be defined as in (
4.48
). Then

\left|K^{\pm}_{33}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.57)

Proof.

Following the method as
K^{\pm,0}_{r}
in Proposition
4.6
, it suffices to prove that the following key oscillatory integrals satisfy the estimate (
4.57
):

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{33}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{r_{3}}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\big]}\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{33}(t,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{r_{3}}e^{-it\big[(2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+})^{2}-s\theta_{+}\big]}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}(\mu(\theta_{+}),m)d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,3}_{33}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(a_{2}(\mu))^{2}\mu^{-3}\widetilde{\Phi}_{3}(\mu,n,m)d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-m_{1}
,
M_{2}=m-m_{2}
,
r_{3}
is defined in Proposition
4.12
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})(\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mu)v)(m_{1}),\quad\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}(\mu,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})(\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}(\mu)(v(\cdot)e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|}))(m_{1}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{3}(\mu,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}v(m_{1})e^{b(\mu)|n-m_{1}|}\Big(\Gamma^{0}_{1}(2-\mu)(v(\cdot)e^{b(\mu)|m-\cdot|})\Big)(m_{1}),

with

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(\mu)=\mu^{-3}(2+\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}(\mu),\quad\widetilde{\varphi}_{2}(\mu)=\mu^{-3}a_{2}(\mu)\Gamma^{0}_{1}(2-\mu),\quad\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}(\mu)=\frac{\Gamma^{0}_{1}(2-\mu)}{\sqrt{2-\mu}}.

Combining (
3.8
) and the facts

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}(\theta_{+})=-\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}},\quad\|\widetilde{\Gamma}^{(k)}_{1}(\mu)\|_{\mathbb{B}(0,0)}\lesssim(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}-k},\quad k=0,1,

(4.58)

the desired estimates follow from Corollary
3.4
and Van der Corput Lemma.
∎

Hence, the estimate (
4.6
) for the regular case is established by Propositions
4.9
,
4.12
and
4.13
.

4.3.2.
Resonant case

If
16
is the resonance of
H
, as before, it follows from (
1.31
) and (
1.25
) that

K^{\pm}_{3}(t,n,m)=\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{11}\cup\mathcal{B}_{12}}K^{\pm}_{B}(t,n,m),

(4.59)

where sets
\mathcal{B}_{1j}
are defined as follows:

\mathcal{B}_{11}=\{(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0},\ \widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01},\ B^{\pm,1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}\},\quad\mathcal{B}_{12}=\{\widetilde{Q}B^{\pm,1}_{03}\widetilde{Q},\ (2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,1}_{1},\ \Gamma^{1}_{1}(2-\mu)\},

and

K^{\pm}_{B}(t,n,m)=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vBvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(4.60)

From Propositions
4.9
,
4.12
and
4.13
, it suffices to establish the estimate (
4.6
) for
K^{\pm}_{B}
with
B\in\mathcal{B}_{11}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>11
. Assume that
16
is a resonance of
H
, then for any
B\in\mathcal{B}_{11}
, we have

\left|K^{\pm}_{B}(t,n,m)\right|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.61)

Proof.

By symmetry, we focus on operators
B=(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0},\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01}
only. Denote

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{-1}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle:=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\pm}_{01}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle:=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

Substituting
\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}
in (
4.3.1
) with
\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}
and
\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01}
, these two kernels can further expressed as

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{-1}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-1}\big(\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{-1}(\mu)\big)(n,m)d\mu,

(4.62)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\pm}_{01}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{-1}\big(\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{01}(\mu)\big)(n,m)d\mu,

(4.63)

where
\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{-1}(\mu)
and
\Lambda^{\pm,j}_{01}(\mu)
are defined in (
4.53
) by replacing
\widetilde{Q}B_{01}\widetilde{Q}
with
\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}
and
\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm,1}_{01}
, respectively.
Since
\big<\widetilde{S}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\big>=0,\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}=0
, Lemma
4.10
is also valid for
\widetilde{S}_{0}
. Basing on Proposition
4.9
, it ultimately reduces to estimate the following key oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{-1}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|N_{1}|+|M_{2}|)}(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-1}\Big(\frac{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\Big)^{2}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{-1}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|M_{2}|}(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-1}\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)\frac{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{N}_{1}|}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,3}_{-1}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-1}(\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu))^{2}e^{b(\mu)(|\widetilde{N}_{1}|+|\widetilde{M}_{2}|)}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,1}_{01}(t,N_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|N_{1}|+|\widetilde{M}_{2}|)}\mu^{-1}\frac{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}{{\rm sin}^{2}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm,2}_{01}(t,\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}\mu^{-1}\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)\frac{1}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{N}_{1}|}d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},\widetilde{N}_{1}=n-m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
. Since the variable substitution (
4.55
) contributes a factor
(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we apply it to these five integrals, yielding the desired result by Corollary
3.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.3.3.
Eigenvalue case

In this scenario, due to the high singularity, our approach will differ slightly from the previous two cases. First, it follows from (
1.31
) and (
(vi)
) that

(K^{+}_{3}-K^{-}_{3})(t,n,m)=(\widetilde{K}_{1}+\widetilde{K}^{+}_{2}-\widetilde{K}^{-}_{2})(t,n,m),

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{1}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{-1}\mu^{3}\Big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})

\displaystyle\quad-R^{-}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{-}_{0}(\mu^{4})\Big](n,m)d\mu,

(4.64)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\pm}_{2}(t,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{2}}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}\mu^{3}\big[R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vBvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

with

\mathcal{B}_{2}=\big\{(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{\pm}_{-1,1}\widetilde{Q},\ (2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{Q}B^{\pm}_{-1,2}\widetilde{S}_{0},\ \widetilde{Q}B^{\pm,2}_{01},\ B^{\pm,2}_{02}\widetilde{Q},\ (2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\pm,2}_{1},\ \Gamma^{2}_{1}(2-\mu)\big\}.

From Propositions
4.9
,
4.12
,
4.13
and
4.14
, the estimate (
4.6
) for
K^{+}_{3}-K^{-}_{3}
finally reduces to
\widetilde{K}_{1}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>15
. Assume that
16
is an eigenvalue of
H
, then the following estimate hold:

\big|\widetilde{K}_{1}(t,n,m)\big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad t\neq 0,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.65)

Proof.

To this end, we employ a trick by adding and substracting a term to obtain

\displaystyle R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})-R^{-}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{-}_{0}(\mu^{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big((R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big)v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})+R^{-}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}v\big((R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big).

Furthermore, by (
4.64
) and symmetry, it suffices to establish the estimate (
4.65
) for
\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{1}
, where

\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{1}(t,n,m)=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{-1}\mu^{3}\big[((R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4}))v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

Using (
4.50
), (
2.10
) and
J^{2}=I
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{1}(t,n,m)=\frac{1}{4}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}(2-\mu)^{-1}\mu^{-1}(\Lambda_{1j})(\mu)(n,m)d\mu,

(4.66)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda_{11}(\mu)

\displaystyle=J\big((R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta})(4-\mu^{2})\big)\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}R^{-}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})J,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{12}(\mu)

\displaystyle=J\big((R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta})(4-\mu^{2})\big)\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}vR_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2}).

From (
4.54
) and the following Lemma
4.16
, it reduces to estimate these two oscillatory integrals:

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{11}(t,N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|M_{2}|\pm|N_{1}|)}(2-\mu)^{-1}\mu^{-1}\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{3}_{+}}{{\rm sin}^{2}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}d\mu,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{12}(t,N_{1},\widetilde{M}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{2-\mu_{0}}^{2}e^{-it\mu^{4}}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|N_{1}|}(2-\mu)^{-1}\widetilde{a_{2}}(\mu)\mu^{-1}\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{+}}{{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}e^{b(\mu)|\widetilde{M}_{2}|}d\mu,

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1},M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2},\widetilde{M}_{2}=m-m_{2}
. Applying the variable substitution (
4.55
) to these integrals and then using Corollary
3.4
, the desired estimate is obtained.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\widetilde{S}_{1}
be as in Definition
1.5
and
\tilde{v}=Jv
. For any
f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then we have

(1)

\big[\big((R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta})(4-\mu^{2})\big)\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}f\big](n)=\frac{-\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{+}}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)F(-\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-\rho m|)d\rho\cdot\tilde{v}_{2}(m)(\widetilde{S}_{1}f)(m)
,

(2)

\widetilde{S}_{1}\big(\tilde{v}\big((R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta})(4-\mu^{2})\big)f\big)=\frac{-\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{+}}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\widetilde{S}_{1}\big(\tilde{v}_{2}(\cdot)\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)F(-\tilde{\theta}_{+}|m-\cdot\rho|)d\rho f(m)\big)
,

where
\tilde{\theta}_{+}
satisfies
{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1
with
\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
and
F(s)=i(e^{-is}+e^{is})
.

Proof.

From (
4.52
), we have

(R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta}\big)(4-\mu^{2},n,m)=\frac{1}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}F(-\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-m|).

Combining
F^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\big<\widetilde{S}_{1}f,\tilde{v}_{k}\big>=0
and
\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}_{k}=0
for
k=0,1
, the desired conclusion can be obtained by following the method in the proof of Lemma
4.2
(2).

∎

5.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

This section is devoted to presenting the proof of Theorem
1.8
. As preliminary steps, we first establish several auxiliary lemmas. To begin with, we give asymptotic behaviors of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
in some suitable weighted space
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
N
be an integer and
\mu\in(0,2)
.

(i)

Suppose that
N\geq-3
and
s>\frac{1}{2}+N+4
, then

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=\sum\limits_{j=-3}^{N}\mu^{j}G^{\pm}_{j}+r^{\pm}_{N}(\mu),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0\ {\rm in}\ \mathbb{B}(s,-s),

(5.1)

where
\left\|r^{\pm}_{N}(\mu)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(s,-s)}=O\left(\mu^{N+1}\right)
as
\mu\rightarrow 0

and
G^{\pm}_{j}
are integral operators with kernels given as follows:

•

G^{\pm}_{-3}(n,m)=\frac{-1\pm i}{4}
,
G^{\pm}_{-2}(n,m)=0
,

•

G^{\pm}_{-1}(n,m)=\frac{1\pm i}{4}\left(\frac{1}{8}-\frac{1}{2}|n-m|^{2}\right):=\frac{1\pm i}{4}{\bm{G_{-1}}}(n,m)
,

•

G^{\pm}_{0}(n,m)=\frac{1}{12}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right):={\bm{G_{0}}}(n,m)
,

•

G^{\pm}_{1}(n,m)=\frac{-1\pm i}{32}\big(\frac{1}{3}|n-m|^{4}-\frac{5}{6}|n-m|^{2}+\frac{3}{16}\big):=\frac{-1\pm i}{32}{\bm{G_{1}}}(n,m)
,

•

G^{\pm}_{2}(n,m)=0
,
G^{\pm}_{3}(n,m)=\frac{-1\mp i}{4\cdot 6!}\big(|n-m|^{6}-\frac{35}{4}|n-m|^{4}+\frac{259}{16}|n-m|^{2}-\frac{225}{64}\big):=\frac{-1\mp i}{4\cdot 6!}{\bm{G_{3}}}(n,m)
,

•

for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{j+3}c^{\pm}_{k,j}|n-m|^{k},\quad c_{k,j}\in\mathbb{C}.

(5.2)

Moreover, in the same sense, the (
5.1
) can be differentiated
N+4
times in
\mu
.

(ii)

Suppose that
N\geq-1
and
s>\frac{1}{2}+N+2
, then

JR^{\pm}_{0}((2-\mu)^{4})J=\sum\limits_{j=-1}^{N}\mu^{\frac{j}{2}}\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}+\mathcal{E}^{\pm}_{N}(\mu),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0\ {\rm in}\ \mathbb{B}(s,-s),

(5.3)

where
\left\|\mathcal{E}^{\pm}_{N}(\mu)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(s,-s)}=O\left(\mu^{\frac{N+1}{2}}\right)
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
and
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}
are integral operators with kernels given as follows:

•

\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{-1}(n,m)=\frac{\pm i}{32}:=\pm i{\bm{\widetilde{G}_{-1}}}(n,m)
,

•

\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{0}(n,m)=\frac{1}{32\sqrt{2}}\big(2\sqrt{2}|n-m|-{(2\sqrt{2}-3)}^{|n-m|}\big):={\bm{\widetilde{G}_{0}}}(n,m)
,

•

\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{1}(n,m)=\frac{\mp i}{32}\big(2|n-m|^{2}-\frac{13}{8}\big):=\pm i{\bm{\widetilde{G}_{1}}}(n,m)
,

•

\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{2}(n,m)=-\frac{1}{24}|n-m|^{3}+\frac{5}{48}|n-m|-\big(2\sqrt{2}-3\big)^{|n-m|}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}|n-m|-\frac{1}{8}+\frac{15}{256\sqrt{2}}\Big):={\bm{\widetilde{G}_{2}}}(n,m)
,

•

for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{j+1}d^{\pm}_{k,j}(n,m)|n-m|^{k},\ d^{\pm}_{j+1,j}(n,m)=\frac{(\mp 2i)^{j}}{16(j+1)!}.

(5.4)

Furthermore, in the same sense, the (
5.3
) can be differentiated
N+2
times in
\mu
.

Proof.

To begin, we recall from (
2.8
) that

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\left(\frac{\pm ie^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}-\frac{e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}\right),

then by Euler’s formula,

\displaystyle e^{\mp i\theta_{+}}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\pm i\mu\Big(1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad e^{b(\mu)}=1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu\Big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.5)

By means of Taylor’s expansion near
\mu=0
, we can obtain the following formal expansion:

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-3}^{+\infty}\mu^{j}G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

with
G^{\pm}_{j}(n,m)
defined in (
5.2
). As for
\mu\rightarrow 2
, we substitute
\mu
with
2-\omega
in (
5.5
) obtaining that

\displaystyle e^{\mp i\theta_{+}}

\displaystyle=-\Big(1-2\omega+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\mp 2i\omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(1-\frac{5\omega}{4}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}-\frac{\omega^{3}}{16}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big),

\displaystyle e^{b(\mu)}

\displaystyle=3\Big(1-\frac{2\omega}{3}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{6}-\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}\Big(1-\frac{3\omega}{2}+\frac{7\omega^{2}}{8}-\frac{\omega^{3}}{4}+\frac{\omega^{4}}{32}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).

Combining the Taylor’s expansion near
\omega=0
with the relation
(-1)^{|n-m|}=(-1)^{n+m}
, the formal expansion can be also derived:

(JR^{\pm}_{0}(2-\mu)^{4}J)(n,m)\thicksim\sum\limits_{j=-1}^{+\infty}\mu^{\frac{j}{2}}\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}(n,m),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

with
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}_{j}
defined in (
5.4
). In what follows, we claim that the formal expansions above hold in the space
\mathbb{B}(s,-s)
for suitable
s
. We only deal with (i) since (ii) can follow in a similar way. Given that
N\geq-3
and
s>\frac{1}{2}+N+4
. Firstly, by Taylor’s expansion with remainders, one obtains that

r^{\pm}_{N}(\mu,n,m)=\mu^{N+1}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N+4}a^{\pm}_{k}(\mu)|n-m|^{k},

where
a^{\pm}_{k}(\mu)=O(1)
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
. Since that
s>\frac{1}{2}+N+4
and
|n-m|^{2k}\lesssim\left<n\right>^{2k}\left<m\right>^{2k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<n\right>^{-{2s}}|n-m|^{2(N+4)}\left<m\right>^{-{2s}}<\infty,

then it follows that
\left\|r^{\pm}_{N}(\mu)\right\|_{\mathbb{B}(s,-s)}=O\left(\mu^{N+1}\right)
. Regarding the differentiability, noting that for each differentiation of (
2.8
), we can obtain a power of
|n-m|
. Therefore, repeating the process above, we can get the desired conclusion.
∎

The following second lemma further gives a characterization on resonances types of
H
from the invertibilities of operators, which provides a direct approach to computing
\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}
via Von-Neumann series expansion. For brevity, we denote the kernel of the operator
T
restricted to the space
X
by
{\rm Ker}T\big|_{X}=\{f\in X:Tf=0\}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
G_{-1},G_{0},G_{1},G_{3}
and
\widetilde{G}_{0},\widetilde{G}_{1},\widetilde{G}_{2}
be integral operators with kernels defined in (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), respectively. Let
T_{0},\widetilde{T}_{0}
be defined as in (
1.20
). Then the following statements hold:

(1)

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}QvG_{-1}vQ\big|_{Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(5.6)

(2)
Denote by
D_{0}
the inverse of
QvG_{-1}vQ+S_{0}
on
Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and define

\displaystyle T_{1}=S_{1}vG_{1}vS_{1}+\frac{8}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}S_{1}vG_{-1}vPvG_{-1}vS_{1}+64S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}T_{0}S_{1},

(5.7)

then

{\rm Ker}T_{1}\big|_{S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(3)
Denote by
D_{2}
the inverse of
T_{1}+S_{2}
on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and let

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6!}\Big(S_{2}vG_{3}vS_{2}-\frac{8\cdot 6!}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}S_{2}{(T_{0})}^{2}S_{2}-\frac{6!}{64}S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{64}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\big(S_{2}T_{0}vG_{-1}vD_{0}-\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{8}S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}T_{0}D_{0}\big)D_{2}

(5.8)

\displaystyle\quad\times\big(D_{0}vG_{-1}vT_{0}S_{2}-\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{8}D_{0}T_{0}D_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}\big),

then

{\rm Ker}T_{2}\big|_{S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(4)
Define

\widetilde{T}_{1}=\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\frac{32}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}\widetilde{S}_{0}(\widetilde{T}_{0})^{2}\widetilde{S}_{0},

(5.9)

then

{\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{1}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(5)
Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}
and
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
with
\beta>9
. Define
\widetilde{T}_{2}=\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}
, then

S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\},\quad{\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{2}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\{0\}.

Remark 5.3
.

Basing on Theorem
1.7
and the observation that

S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})={\rm ker}S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}\big|_{S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})},\quad\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})={\rm Ker}\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}\widetilde{Q}\big|_{\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})},

this lemma further indicates the following characterizations on resonances types of
H
:

(i) 0 is a regular point of
H
if and only if
S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
is invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii) 0 is a first kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
is not invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but
T_{1}
is invertible on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii) 0 is a second kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
is not invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
T_{1}
is not invertible on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but
T_{2}
is invertible on
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iv) 16 is a regular point of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}\widetilde{Q}
is invertible on
\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(v) 16 is a resonance of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}\widetilde{Q}
is not invertible on
\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but
\widetilde{T}_{1}
is invertible on
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(vi) 16 is an eigenvalue of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}\widetilde{Q}
is not invertible on
\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
\widetilde{T}_{1}
is not invertible on
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but
\widetilde{T}_{2}
is invertible on
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The proof of this lemma will be delayed to the end of this section. Furthermore, the following lemma will be frequently used in the proof.

Lemma 5.4
.

[
31
, Lemma 2.1]
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space. Let
A
be a closed operator and
S
a projection. Suppose
A+S
has a bounded inverse. Then
A
has a bounded inverse if and only if

a\equiv S-S(A+S)^{-1}S

has a bounded inverse in
S\mathcal{H}
, and in this case

A^{-1}=(A+S)^{-1}+(A+S)^{-1}Sa^{-1}S(A+S)^{-1}.

With these three lemmas, we begin the proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

(i)
{\bm{0}}
is the regular point of
H
and
\bm{\beta>15}
.

Taking
N=3
in (
5.1
), namely, as
s>\frac{15}{2}
, we have the resolvent expansion

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=\mu^{-3}G^{\pm}_{-3}+\mu^{-1}G^{\pm}_{-1}+G^{\pm}_{0}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{3}\mu^{k}G^{\pm}_{k}+\Gamma_{4}(\mu)\ \ {\rm in}\ \mathbb{B}(s,-s),\ \mu\rightarrow 0.

Since
\beta>15
and
M^{\pm}(\mu)=U+vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})v
, we obtain the following relation on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle M^{\pm}(\mu)=\frac{a^{\pm}}{\mu^{3}}\Big(P+\frac{\mu^{2}}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{-1}v+\frac{\mu^{3}}{a^{\pm}}T_{0}+\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{3}\mu^{k+3}vG^{\pm}_{k}v+\Gamma_{7}(\mu)\Big):=\frac{a^{\pm}}{\mu^{3}}\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu),

where we used the fact that
vG^{\pm}_{-3}v=a^{\pm}P
with
a^{\pm}=\frac{-1\pm i}{4}\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}
. Then

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\frac{\mu^{3}}{a^{\pm}}\big(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}.

(5.10)

Step1:
By Lemma
5.4
,
\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)
is invertible on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Leftrightarrow M^{\pm}_{1}(\mu):=Q-Q\big(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)+Q\big)^{-1}Q
is invertible on
Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and in this case, one has

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\big(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)+Q\big)^{-1}\Big[I+Q\left(M^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)\right)^{-1}Q\big(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)+Q\big)^{-1}\Big],

(5.11)

where

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{M}^{\pm}(\mu)+Q\big)^{-1}=I-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{5}\mu^{k+1}B^{\pm}_{k}+\Gamma_{7}(\mu),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

(5.12)

with

•

B^{\pm}_{1}=\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{-1}v
,
B^{\pm}_{2}=\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}T_{0}
,
B^{\pm}_{3}=\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{1}v-\left(\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{-1}v\right)^{2}
,

•

B^{\pm}_{4}=-\left(\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\left(vG^{\pm}_{-1}vT_{0}+T_{0}vG^{\pm}_{-1}v\right)
,

•

B^{\pm}_{5}=\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{3}v-\left(\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\left(vG^{\pm}_{-1}v\cdot vG^{\pm}_{1}v+(T_{0})^{2}+vG^{\pm}_{1}v\cdot vG^{\pm}_{-1}v\right)+\left(\frac{1}{a^{\pm}}vG^{\pm}_{-1}v\right)^{3}
.

This yields

\displaystyle M^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)=b^{\pm}\mu^{2}\Big(QvG_{-1}vQ+\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{5}\mu^{k-1}QB^{\pm}_{k}Q+Q\Gamma_{5}(\mu)Q\Big):=b^{\pm}\mu^{2}\widetilde{M}^{\pm}_{1}(\mu),

where we used the fact that
QB^{\pm}_{1}Q=\frac{a^{\pm}_{-1}}{a^{\pm}}QvG_{-1}vQ:=b^{\pm}QvG_{-1}vQ
with
a^{\pm}_{-1}=\frac{1\pm i}{4}
. Hence,

\left(M^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{b^{\pm}\mu^{2}}\left(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}.

(5.13)

Since
{\rm Ker}QvG_{-1}vQ\big|_{Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then by Lemma
5.4
, we have

Step2:

\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)
is invertible on
Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Leftrightarrow M^{\pm}_{2}(\mu):=S_{0}-S_{0}\big(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{0}\big)^{-1}S_{0}
is invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. In this case,

\big(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}=\big(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{0}\big)^{-1}\Big(I+S_{0}\big(M^{\pm}_{2}(\mu)\big)^{-1}S_{0}\big(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{0}\big)^{-1}\Big),

(5.14)

where

\left(\widetilde{M}_{1}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{0}\right)^{-1}=D_{0}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{4}\mu^{k}\widetilde{B}_{k}^{\pm}+D_{0}\Gamma_{5}(\mu)D_{0},\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

(5.15)

with
D_{0}
being the inverse of
QvG_{-1}vQ+S_{0}
on
Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
(hence
D_{0}Q=D_{0}=QD_{0}
) and

•

\widetilde{B}_{1}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}
,
\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}D_{0}-\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}\right)^{2}D_{0}
,

•

\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{4}D_{0}-\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}D_{0}+D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}\right)^{3}D_{0},

•

\widetilde{B}_{4}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{5}D_{0}-\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{4}D_{0}+\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}\right)^{2}D_{0}+D_{0}B^{\pm}_{4}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}\right)

\quad\ \ +\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}\right)^{3}\left(\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}\right)^{2}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}D_{0}+D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}D_{0}+D_{0}B^{\pm}_{3}\left(D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}\right)^{2}D_{0}\right)

\quad\ \ -\left(\frac{1}{b^{\pm}}D_{0}B^{\pm}_{2}\right)^{4}D_{0}
.

Using
S_{0}D_{0}=S_{0}=S_{0}D_{0}
and
S_{0}\widetilde{B}_{1}^{\pm}S_{0}=\frac{1}{a^{\pm}_{-1}}S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle M^{\pm}_{2}(\mu)=\frac{\mu}{a^{\pm}_{-1}}\Big(S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}+a^{\pm}_{-1}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{4}\mu^{k-1}S_{0}\widetilde{B}_{k}^{\pm}S_{0}+S_{0}\Gamma_{4}(\mu)S_{0}\Big):=\frac{\mu}{a^{\pm}_{-1}}\widetilde{M}^{\pm}_{2}(\mu),

and thus

\left(M^{\pm}_{2}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\frac{a^{\pm}_{-1}}{\mu}\left(\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)\right)^{-1}.

(5.16)

Since
0
is a regular point of
H
, then by Remark
5.3
,
S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
is invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Denote its inverse by
D_{1}
, then using Von-Neumann expansion and
S_{0}D_{1}=D_{1}=D_{1}S_{0}
, one has

\displaystyle\left(M^{\pm}_{2}(\mu)\right)^{-1}=\frac{a^{\pm}_{-1}}{\mu}D_{1}+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2}\widetilde{D}^{\pm}_{k}\mu^{k}+D_{1}\Gamma_{3}(\mu)D_{1},\quad\mu\rightarrow 0,

(5.17)

with

•

\widetilde{D}^{\pm}_{0}=-\left(a^{\pm}_{-1}\right)^{2}D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}D_{1}
,
\widetilde{D}^{\pm}_{1}=-\big(a^{\pm}_{-1}\big)^{2}\big(D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\pm}D_{1}-a^{\pm}_{-1}\big(D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}\big)^{2}D_{1}\big)
,

•

\widetilde{D}^{\pm}_{2}=-\big(a^{\pm}_{-1}\big)^{2}D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{4}^{\pm}D_{1}+\big(a^{\pm}_{-1}\big)^{3}\big(D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\pm}D_{1}+D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\pm}D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}D_{1}\big)

-\big(a^{\pm}_{-1}\big)^{4}\big(D_{1}\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\pm}\big)^{3}D_{1}
.

Taking (
5.17
) into (
5.14
),(
5.13
),(
5.11
) and (
5.10
) in order, the desired (
1.21
) is obtained.

(ii)
{\bm{0}}
is the first kind resonance of
H
and
\bm{\beta>19}
.
For this case, we take
N=5
in (
5.1
) and repeat
Step1
to
Step2
in
(i)
. However, since
S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}
is not invertible in
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we need to further analyze the
\widetilde{M}^{\pm}_{2}(\mu)
in
Step2
of
(i)
. By virtue of
{\rm Ker}S_{0}T_{0}S_{0}\big|_{S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and Lemma
5.4
, we proceed with

Step3:

\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)
is invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Leftrightarrow M^{\pm}_{3}(\mu):=S_{1}-S_{1}\big(\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{1}\big)^{-1}S_{1}
is invertible on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. In this case,

\big(\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)\big)^{-1}=\big(\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{1}\big)^{-1}\Big(I+S_{1}\big(M^{\pm}_{3}(\mu)\big)^{-1}S_{1}\big(\widetilde{M}_{2}^{\pm}(\mu)+S_{1}\big)^{-1}\Big),

(5.18)

where
