Finally, we present the proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

(1)
For any
f\in Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, by virtue of
\left<f,v\right>=0
and the expression

G_{-1}(n,m)=\frac{1}{8}-\frac{1}{2}|n-m|^{2},

a direct calculation yields that

QvG_{-1}vQf=\left<f,v_{1}\right>Q(v_{1}).

(5.40)

Since
Q(v_{1})\not\equiv 0
, it implies that

g\in{\rm Ker}QvG_{-1}vQ\big|_{Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\Leftrightarrow\left<g,v\right>=0\ {\rm and}\ \left<g,v_{1}\right>=0\Leftrightarrow g\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(2)
It is key to calculate that for any
f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle T_{1}f=4\big<f,v_{2}\big>S_{1}(v_{2})+64\frac{\big<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}S_{1}(T_{0}v^{\prime}),\quad v^{\prime}=Q(v_{1})=v_{1}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v.

(5.41)

Once this is established, then “
\supseteq"
is obviously and “
\subseteq
” can be obtained by the following relation:

0=\big<T_{1}f,f\big>=4\left|\big<f,v_{2}\big>\right|^{2}+64\frac{\left|\big<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\big>\right|^{2}}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}.

To obtain (
5.41
), we divide several steps to calculate.

i)
By virtue of the property that
\big<S_{1}f,v_{k}\big>=0
,
S_{1}v_{k}=0
for
k=0,1
and the kernels:

\displaystyle G_{-1}(n,m)=\frac{1}{8}-\frac{1}{2}|n-m|^{2},\quad G_{1}(n,m)=\frac{1}{3}|n-m|^{4}-\frac{5}{6}|n-m|^{2}+\frac{3}{16},

it is easy to verify that

S_{1}vG_{1}vS_{1}f=2\big<f,v_{2}\big>S_{1}v_{2},\quad S_{1}vG_{-1}vPvG_{-1}vS_{1}f=\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{4}\big<f,v_{2}\big>S_{1}v_{2}.

(5.42)

ii)
Let
v^{\prime}=Qv_{1}
. By
S_{0}v^{\prime}=0
, (
5.40
) and
\big<v,v^{\prime}\big>=0
, it yields that

(QvG_{-1}vQ+S_{0})v^{\prime}=\big<v^{\prime},v_{1}\big>Q(v_{1})=\big<v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\big>v^{\prime},

which implies that

D_{0}v^{\prime}=\frac{v^{\prime}}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}.

(5.43)

Then basing on
D_{0}P=0
and
S_{0}T_{0}f=0
, we have

\displaystyle S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}T_{0}S_{1}f=S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}(P+Q-S_{0}+S_{0})T_{0}f=S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}(Q-S_{0})T_{0}f=\frac{\big<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}S_{1}(T_{0}v^{\prime}),

(5.44)

where in the last equality we used the facts that
(Q-S_{0})\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})={\rm Span}\{v^{\prime}\}
and (
5.43
). Therefore, combining (
5.42
), (
5.44
) with (
5.7
), (
5.41
) is derived.

(3)
Firstly, we can verify that for any
f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle T_{2}f

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6!}\Big(40\big<f,v_{3}\big>S_{2}(v_{3})+\frac{8\cdot 6!}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\big<T_{0}f,v\big>S_{2}T_{0}v\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{64}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{4}\big<T_{0}f,v\big>\big<D_{2}S_{1}v_{2},S_{1}v_{2}\big>-\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{12}\frac{\big<f,v_{3}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime},S_{1}v_{2}\big>\right)S_{2}T_{0}v\right.

(5.45)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-\left.\left(\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{12}\frac{\big<T_{0}f,v\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}v_{2},S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime}\big>-\frac{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}{36}\frac{\big<f,v_{3}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime},S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime}\big>\right)S_{2}(v_{3})\right].

Assuming that (
5
) holds (prove later), then “
\supseteq"
is obviously. However, as for “
\subseteq
”, we need do more analysis. Let

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\big<f,v_{3}\big>=X_{1}+iX_{2},\quad Y:=\big<T_{0}f,v\big>=Y_{1}+iY_{2},\quad X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=S_{1}v_{2},\quad h_{1}=D_{2}g_{1},\quad g_{2}=S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime},\quad h_{2}=D_{2}g_{2},

and then

\displaystyle 0=\big<T_{2}f,f\big>=a(X^{2}_{1}+X^{2}_{2})+b(Y^{2}_{1}+Y^{2}_{2})-dc_{1}(X_{1}Y_{1}+X_{2}Y_{2})+dc_{2}(X_{2}Y_{1}-X_{1}Y_{2}),

(5.46)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}a&=\frac{1}{9}\Big(\frac{16\big<h_{2},g_{2}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big),\quad b=\frac{8}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\big(2\big<h_{1},g_{1}\big>-1\big),\\
d&=\frac{32}{3\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}},\quad c_{1}+ic_{2}=\big<h_{2},g_{1}\big>,\quad c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

(5.47)

Next we consider introducing the following real quadratic form:

F(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}):=a(x^{2}_{1}+x^{2}_{2})+b(x^{2}_{3}+x^{2}_{4})-dc_{1}(x_{1}x_{3}+x_{2}x_{4})+dc_{2}(x_{2}x_{3}-x_{1}x_{4}),

with coefficients
a,b,d,c_{1},c_{2}
defined in (
5.47
). Then we can verify that

a<0,\quad 4ab-d^{2}(c^{2}_{1}+c^{2}_{2})>0.

(5.48)

which implies that
F
is a negative definite quadratic form. Thus, it follows from (
5.46
) that
X=Y=0
, which establishes “
\subseteq
”. To obtain (
5.48
), noting that
h_{i}\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then by (
5.41
),

\displaystyle g_{i}=D^{-1}_{2}h_{i}=(T_{1}+S_{2})h_{i}=4\big<h_{i},g_{1}\big>g_{1}+64\frac{\big<h_{i},g_{2}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}g_{2}+S_{2}h_{i},\quad i=1,2

Then it concludes that

\big<g_{1},h_{1}\big>\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad\big<g_{2},h_{2}\big>\leq\frac{1}{64}\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}},

and both inequalities are strict if
\big<h_{2},g_{1}\big>\neq 0
.
Thus,
a<0,b<0
. Obviously,
d^{2}(c^{2}_{1}+c^{2}_{2})-4ab<0
when
\big<h_{2},g_{1}\big>=0
. If
\big<h_{2},g_{1}\big>\neq 0
, one can calculate that

\displaystyle d^{2}(c^{2}_{1}+c^{2}_{2})-4ab

\displaystyle=\frac{32^{2}}{9\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\Big(\frac{\big|\big<g_{2},h_{1}\big>\big|^{2}}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}-\frac{\big<h_{2},g_{2}\big>\big<h_{1},g_{1}\big>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}+\frac{\big<h_{2},g_{2}\big>}{2\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}+\frac{\big<h_{1},g_{1}\big>}{32}-\frac{1}{64}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{32^{2}}{9\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\Big(\frac{\big<h_{2},g_{2}\big>}{2\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}+\frac{\big<h_{1},g_{1}\big>}{32}-\frac{1}{64}\Big)<0,

where in the first inequality we used the fact

\big|\big<g_{2},h_{1}\big>\big|^{2}=\big|\big<g_{2},D_{2}g_{1}\big>\big|^{2}\leq\big<g_{2},D_{2}g_{2}\big>\big<g_{1},D_{2}g_{1}\big>=\big<h_{2},g_{2}\big>\big<h_{1},g_{1}\big>.

Now we come to prove (
5
).

i)
By virtue of
\big<S_{2}f,v_{k}\big>=0
,
S_{2}v_{k}=0
for
k=0,1,2
and the kernel

G_{3}(n,m)=|n-m|^{6}-\frac{35}{4}|n-m|^{4}+\frac{259}{16}|n-m|^{2}-\frac{225}{64},

it is not difficult to verify that

S_{2}vG_{3}vS_{2}f=-20\big<f,v_{3}\big>S_{2}v_{3}.

(5.49)

Noticing that
QT_{0}f=0
,
QD_{0}=D_{0}=D_{0}Q
and basing on (
5.43
), one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{2}{(T_{0})}^{2}S_{2}f&=S_{2}T_{0}(P+Q)T_{0}f=S_{2}T_{0}PT_{0}f=\frac{\big<T_{0}f,v\big>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}S_{2}T_{0}v,\\
S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}f&=S_{2}vG_{1}vQD_{0}QvG_{1}vf=\frac{16}{9}\big<f,v_{3}\big>S_{2}v_{3}.\end{split}

(5.50)

ii)
To calculate the second term in (
5.2
), noticing that
D_{0}D_{2}=D_{2}=D_{0}D_{2}
and
D_{2}=S_{1}D_{2}S_{1}
, it reduces to the following four operators. By virtue of the cancelation of
S_{1},S_{2}
and (
5.43
), it is not difficult to obtain that

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{2}T_{0}vG_{-1}vS_{1}D_{2}S_{1}vG_{-1}vT_{0}S_{2}f&=\frac{1}{4}\big<T_{0}f,v\big>\big<D_{2}S_{1}v_{2},S_{1}v_{2}\big>S_{2}T_{0}v,\\
S_{2}T_{0}vG_{-1}vS_{1}D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}f&=\frac{2\big<f,v_{3}\big>}{3\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime},S_{1}v_{2}\big>S_{2}T_{0}v,\\
S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}T_{0}S_{1}D_{2}S_{1}vG_{-1}vT_{0}S_{2}f&=\frac{2\big<T_{0}f,v\big>}{3\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}v_{2},S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime}\big>S_{2}v_{3},\\
S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}T_{0}S_{1}D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}D_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}f&=\frac{16\big<f,v_{3}\big>}{9\|v^{\prime}\|^{4}_{\ell^{2}}}\big<D_{2}S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime},S_{1}T_{0}v^{\prime}\big>S_{2}v_{3}.\end{split}

(5.51)

Thus, combining (
5.49
), (
5.50
), (
5.51
) with (
5.2
), (
5
) is established.

(4)
Similarly, we can calculate that for any
f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{1}f=\frac{1}{8}\big<f,\tilde{v}_{1}\big>\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}_{1}+\frac{32}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\big<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\big>\widetilde{S}_{0}\widetilde{T}_{0}\tilde{v},

and then the desired result is established.

(5)

(a)
By Lemma
6.1
, to verify
S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
, it is equivalent to verify that 0 is not the eigenvalue of
H
, i.e., assuming that
H\phi=0
with
\phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then
\phi=0
. To see this, we consider introducing
f=Uv\phi
, then by Lemma
6.1
,
f\in S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=-(G_{0}vf)(n)=-\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\sum\limits_{m<n}\big((n-m)^{3}-(n-m)\big)V(m)\phi(m),

where in the third equality we used the facts that
\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0
for
k=0,1,2,3
and
v(m)f(m)=V(m)\phi(m)
. Given
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
, for
n\geq n_{0}
, we derive

\displaystyle 6|\phi(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n_{0}\leq m\leq n-1}\big((n-m)^{3}-(n-m)\big)|V(m)\phi(m)|

\displaystyle\quad+\sup\limits_{m<n_{0}}|\phi(m)|\sum\limits_{m<n_{0}}\big((n-m)^{3}-(n-m)\big)|V(m)|,

multiplying both sides by
\left<n\right>^{-3}
yields

\displaystyle\left<n\right>^{-3}|\phi(n)|\leq\sum\limits_{n_{0}\leq m\leq n-1}C\left<m\right>^{3}|V(m)|\cdot|\phi(m)|+C\sup\limits_{m<n_{0}}|\phi(m)|\cdot\|{\left<\cdot\right>}^{3}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},

(5.52)

where
C
is a constant.
Denote

u(n)=\left<n\right>^{-3}|\phi(n)|,\quad B(m)=C\left<m\right>^{6}|V(m)|,\quad A(n_{0})=C\|{\left<\cdot\right>}^{3}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}}\sup\limits_{m<n_{0}}|\phi(m)|,

then (
5.52
) can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle u(n)\leq\sum\limits_{n_{0}\leq m\leq n-1}B(m)u(m)+A(n_{0}):=\omega(n).

(5.53)

Next we prove that
u=0
and this gives that
\phi=0
. Indeed,

i)
If
A(n_{0})>0
, then for any
k\geq n_{0}
, we have

\omega(k+1)=\omega(k)+B(k)u(k)\leq\omega(k)+B(k)\omega(k),

which implies that

{\rm ln}\frac{\omega(n)}{\omega(n_{0})}=\sum\limits_{k=n_{0}}^{n-1}{\rm ln}\frac{\omega(k+1)}{\omega(k)}\leq\sum\limits_{k=n_{0}}^{n-1}{\rm ln}(1+B(k)).

Using the inequality that
1+x\leq e^{x}
for
x\geq 0
, one has

u(n)\leq\omega(n)\leq A(n_{0})e^{\|B(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

(5.54)

Noting that under the decay assumption on
V
, we have
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow-\infty}\phi(n)=0
and thus
u=0
by taking limit
n_{0}\rightarrow-\infty
in the (
5.54
).

ii)
If
A(n_{0})=0
, then
\sup\limits_{m<n_{0}}|\phi(m)|=0
, which indicates that for any
n<n_{0}
,
\phi(n)=0
. On the other hand, by (
5.53
), for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle u(n)\leq\sum\limits_{n_{0}\leq m\leq n-1}B(m)u(m)+\varepsilon:=\tilde{\omega}(n).

(5.55)

Then it follows from
i)
that for any
n\geq n_{0}
,

u(n)\leq\varepsilon e^{\|B(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}}},

which concludes that
u(n)=0
by the arbitrary of
\varepsilon
. Therefore, we obtain that
\phi=0
.

(b)
For any
f\in\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
satisfying
\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}f=0
, then

\displaystyle 0=\big<\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}f,f\big>=\big<\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}f,\tilde{v}f\big>.

On the other hand, by (
5.3
) and

\widetilde{G}_{-1}\tilde{v}f=0,\quad\big<\widetilde{G}_{1}\tilde{v}f,\tilde{v}f\big>=0,

we have

\displaystyle\big<\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}f,\tilde{v}f\big>

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\omega}\big<(R_{0}((2-\omega)^{4})-J\widetilde{G}_{0}J)vf,vf\big>

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\omega}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-(2-\omega)^{4}}-\frac{1}{(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}-16}\Big)|(\mathcal{F}(vf))(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\omega\rightarrow 0}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{((2-\omega)^{2}+4)(\omega-4)}{(16-(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}+(2-\omega)^{4}-16)(16-(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2})}|(\mathcal{F}(vf))(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-32\big<\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f,\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\big>,

where in the second equality we used the Plancherel’s identity and the relation
vf=(\Delta^{2}-16)J\widetilde{G}_{0}Jvf
and the fourth equality is obtained from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem by choosing
|\omega|\leq 1
and Re
((2-\omega)^{4}-16))>0
. Then
\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f=0
, and by Lemma
6.1
,
f=-UvJ\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f=0
, thus
{\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{2}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\{0\}
. This completes the whole proof.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

This section aims to provide the proof of Theorem
1.7
. To this end, we first recall some operators and spaces. Let

T_{0}=U+vG_{0}v,\quad\widetilde{T}_{0}=U+\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v},

where
U(n)={\rm sign}(V(n))
,
v(n)=\sqrt{|V(n)|}
and
G_{0}
,
\widetilde{G}_{0}
are integral operators with the following kernels, respectively:

\displaystyle G_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32\sqrt{2}}\left(2\sqrt{2}|n-m|-\Big(2\sqrt{2}-3\right)^{|n-m|}\Big).

Additionally,
Q,S_{j}(j=0,1,2,3),\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{S}_{0},\widetilde{S}_{1}
(defined as in Definitions
1.4
and
1.5
) are orthogonal projections onto the following spaces:

(i)

Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\left({\rm span}\{v\}\right)^{\bot}
,
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\left({\rm span}\{v,v_{1}\}\right)^{\bot}
,
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):S_{0}T_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(ii)

S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{2}\right>=0,\ QT_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(iii)

S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{3}\right>=0,\ T_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(iv)

\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\left({\rm span}\{\tilde{v}\}\right)^{\bot}
,
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0\big\}
,

(v)

\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):=\big\{f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}_{1}\right>=0,\ \widetilde{T}_{0}f=0\big\}
.

To establish Theorem
1.7
, it suffices to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}
and
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
with
\beta>9
, then

(i)

f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2}
,
where

c_{1}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}},\quad c_{2}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}c_{1},\quad v^{\prime}=Q(v_{1})=v_{1}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v.

(6.1)

(ii)

f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and

\phi=-G_{0}vf+\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

(iii)

f\in S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi=-G_{0}vf
.

(iv)

f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=16\phi
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and

\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+J\frac{\big<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\big>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

(6.2)

(v)

f\in\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=16\phi
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f
.

Remark 6.2
.

As an consequence this lemma, it follows that

\displaystyle S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ no\ nonzero\ solution\ in\ }W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ no\ nonzero\ solution\ in\ }W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ no\ nonzero\ solution\ in\ }\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=16\phi\ {\rm has\ no\ nonzero\ solution\ in\ }W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=16\phi\ {\rm has\ no\ nonzero\ solution\ in\ }\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

which gives Theorem
1.7
.

Proof.

(i)
“
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” Let
f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then
f\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
S_{0}T_{0}f=0
. Denote by
P_{0}
the orthogonal projection onto span
\{v,v_{1}\}
. Then
S_{0}=I-P_{0}
, and it follows that

Uf=-vG_{0}vf+P_{0}T_{0}f.

(6.3)

Let

v^{\prime}=Q(v_{1})=v_{1}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v,

(6.4)

so that
\{v^{\prime},v\}
forms an orthogonal basis for span
\{v,v_{1}\}
. In this case, we have

P_{0}T_{0}f=\frac{\left<P_{0}T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v+\frac{\left<P_{0}T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}v^{\prime}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v+\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}v^{\prime}.

(6.5)

Substituting (
6.4
) into the second equality of (
6.5
), we further obtain that

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{0}T_{0}f&=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v+\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}\left(v_{1}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v\right)\\
&=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}v_{1}+\left(\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}-\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}\right)v\\
&:=c_{1}v_{1}+c_{2}v.\end{split}

(6.6)

Multiplying both sides of (
6.3
) by
U
and substituting
P_{0}T_{0}f
with (
6.6
), then

f=-UvG_{0}vf+U(c_{1}v_{1}+c_{2}v)=Uv(-G_{0}vf+c_{1}n+c_{2}):=Uv\phi.

Firstly,
\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2}\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Considering that
|c_{1}n+c_{2}|\lesssim 1+|n|\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
, it suffices to verify that
G_{0}vf\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, since
\left<f,v\right>=0
and
\left<f,v_{1}\right>=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle 12(G_{0}vf)(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|-n^{2}|n|+3|n|nm\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}K_{1}(n,m)v(m)f(m).

We decompose
K_{1}(n,m)
into three parts:

\displaystyle K_{1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=|n-m|(n^{2}-2nm+m^{2}-1)-n^{2}|n|+3|n|nm

\displaystyle=\left(n^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)+|n|nm\right)-2n(|n-m|-|n|)m+(m^{2}-1)|n-m|

\displaystyle:=K_{11}(n,m)-K_{12}(n,m)+K_{13}(n,m).

For
K_{11}(n,m)
, if
n\neq m
, then

\displaystyle|K_{11}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\left(n^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)+|n|nm\right)(|n-m|+|n|)}{|n-m|+|n|}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{n^{2}m^{2}+|n|nm(|n-m|-|n|)}{|n-m|+|n|}\right|\leq 2|n|m^{2}.

Since
K_{11}(n,n)=0
, we always have
|K_{11}(n,m)|\leq 2|n|m^{2}
.
As for
K_{12}(n,m),K_{13}(n,m)
, by the triangle inequality, it yields that

|K_{12}(n,m)|\leq 2|n|m^{2},\quad|K_{13}(n,m)|\leq(1+|n|)|m|^{3}.

In summary, one obtains that
|K_{1}(n,m)|\lesssim(1+|n|)|m|^{3}
. Thus, in view that
\beta>9
,

|(G_{0}vf)(n)|\lesssim\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|K_{1}(n,m)||v(m)f(m)|\lesssim\left<n\right>\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<m\right>^{3}|v(m)||f(m)|\lesssim\left<n\right>\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z}).

Consequently, we conclude that
\phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Next, we show that
H\phi=0
. Notice that
\Delta^{2}G_{0}vf=vf
and
vf=vUv\phi=V\phi
, it yields that

H\phi=(\Delta^{2}+V)\phi=-\Delta^{2}G_{0}vf+V\phi=-vf+vf=0.

“
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” Suppose that
\phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
and satisfies
H\phi=0
. Let
f=Uv\phi
. We will show that
f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and that
\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2}
with
c_{1},c_{2}
defined in (
6.1
).

On one hand
,
f\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, i.e., for
k=0,1
, it can be verified that

\left<f,v_{k}\right>=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(Uv\phi)(n)n^{k}v(n)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n^{k}V(n)\phi(n)=0.

In fact, take
\eta(x)\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\eta(x)=1
for
|x|\leq 1
and
\eta(x)=0
for
|x|>2
. For
k=0,1
and any
\delta>0
, define

F(\delta)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n^{k}V(n)\phi(n)\eta(\delta n).

For one thing, under the assumptions on
V
and
\phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
, it follows from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem that

\left<f,v_{k}\right>=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}F(\delta).

For another, for any
\delta>0
, using the relation
V(n)\phi(n)=-(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)
and
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

F(\delta)=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)n^{k}\eta(\delta n)=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi(n)(\Delta^{2}G_{\delta,k})(n),

where
G_{\delta,k}(x)=x^{k}\eta(\delta x)
. Next we prove that for any
0<\delta<\frac{1}{3}
,
s>0
,

\|\left<\cdot\right>^{s}(\Delta^{2}G_{\delta,k})(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq C(k,s,\eta)\delta^{\frac{7}{2}-k-s},\quad k=0,1,

(6.7)

where
C(k,s,\eta)
is a constant depending on
k,s
and
\eta
. Once this estimate is established, taking
\frac{3}{2}<s_{0}<\frac{5}{2}
, utilizing
\phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
and Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

|F(\delta)|\leq C(k,s,\eta)\delta^{\frac{7}{2}-k-s_{0}}\|\left<\cdot\right>^{-s_{0}}\phi(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})},\quad k=0,1.

This implies
\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}F(\delta)=0
, which proves that
f\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
To derive (
6.7
), we first apply the differential mean value theorem to get

(\Delta^{2}G_{\delta,k})(n)=G^{(4)}_{\delta,k}(n-1+\Theta),

for some
\Theta\in[0,4]
. By Leibniz’s derivative rule and supp
(\eta^{(j)})\subseteq\{x:1\leq|x|\leq 2\}
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, one has

\left|(\Delta^{2}G_{\delta,k})(n)\right|=\left|G^{(4)}_{\delta,k}(n-1+\Theta)\right|\leq C_{k}\delta^{4-k}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{k}\left|\eta^{(4-\ell)}(\delta(n-1+\Theta))\right|.

(6.8)

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, any
s>0
and
0<\delta<\frac{1}{3}
, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\|\left<\cdot\right>^{s}\eta^{(\ell)}(\delta(\cdot-1+\Theta))\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq C(s,\eta,\ell)\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{3}{\delta}}|n|^{2s}\leq C^{\prime}(s,\eta,\ell)\delta^{-2s-1},

which gives the desired (
6.7
) by combining (
6.8
) with the triangle inequality.

On the other hand
, we first show that
\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2}
, from which it follows that

S_{0}T_{0}f=S_{0}(U+vG_{0}v)f=S_{0}v\phi+S_{0}vG_{0}vf=S_{0}v\phi+S_{0}v(-\phi+c_{1}n+c_{2})=S_{0}v\phi-S_{0}v\phi=0.

To see this, since
f\in S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and according to “
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
”,
G_{0}vf\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then
\tilde{\phi}:=\phi+G_{0}vf\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\Delta^{2}\tilde{\phi}=H\phi=0
,
which indicates that
\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{c}_{1}n+\tilde{c}_{2}
for some constants
\tilde{c}_{1}
and
\tilde{c}_{2}
. Next we determine that
\tilde{c}_{1}=c_{1},\tilde{c}_{2}=c_{2}
. Indeed, since

0=H\phi=(\Delta^{2}+V)\phi=-vf-VG_{0}vf+V(\tilde{c}_{1}n+\tilde{c}_{2})=U(-vT_{0}f+\tilde{c}_{1}nv^{2}+\tilde{c}_{2}v^{2}),

then
\tilde{c}_{1}v_{1}+\tilde{c}_{2}v=T_{0}f
. Based on this, we further have

\displaystyle\tilde{c_{1}}\left<v_{1},v\right>+\tilde{c}_{2}\left<v,v\right>

\displaystyle=\left<T_{0}f,v\right>,

(6.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{c_{1}}\left<v_{1},v_{1}\right>+\tilde{c}_{2}\left<v,v_{1}\right>

\displaystyle=\left<T_{0}f,v_{1}\right>,

(6.10)

and combine that
v_{1}=v^{{}^{\prime}}+\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v
and
\big<v^{{}^{\prime}},v\big>=0
, it follows that

\tilde{c}_{1}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}},\quad\tilde{c}_{2}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}\tilde{c}_{1}.

Therefore,
f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and (i) is derived.

(ii)
“
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” Given any
f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then by
(i)
, there exists
\phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
with the following form:

\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2},\ {\rm such\ that\ }H\phi=0\ {\rm and}\ f=Uv\phi,

where
c_{1},c_{2}
are defined as in (
6.1
). Next we show that
c_{1}=0
and
G_{0}vf\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then the desired result is established. Indeed, since
f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, it follows that
QT_{0}f=0
and thus

c_{1}=\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}=\frac{\left<QT_{0}f,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}}=0.

On the other hand, by the definition of
G_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle 12(G_{0}vf)(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|-n^{2}|n|+|n|+3|n|nm-3|n|m^{2}\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}K_{2}(n,m)v(m)f(m),

(6.11)

where the second equality follows from the fact that
\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0
for
k=0,1,2
. For
n\neq 0
, we decompose
K_{2}(n,m)
into four components:

\displaystyle K_{2}(n,m)

\displaystyle=(m^{2}-1)(|n-m|-|n|)+(-2)\big(mn(|n-m|-|n|)+|n|m^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{n^{2}m^{2}}{|n-m|+|n|}-\frac{1}{2}|n|m^{2}\Big)+\Big(\frac{|n|nm(|n-m|-|n|)}{|n-m|+|n|}+\frac{1}{2}|n|m^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{4}K_{2j}(n,m).

(6.12)

Obviously,
|K_{21}(n,m)|\lesssim{\left<m\right>}^{3}
uniformly in
n
, and in what follows, we prove that

|K_{2j}(n,m)|\lesssim{\left<m\right>}^{3},\quad j=2,3,4,\ {\rm uniformly\ in}\ n.

These estimates, combining (
6
), (
6
) and the assumption of
V
yield that
G_{0}vf\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. To see this, by finding the common denominator and triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle|K_{22}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=2\Big|\frac{nm^{3}+|n|m^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)}{|n-m|+|n|}\Big|\lesssim{\left<m\right>}^{3},

\displaystyle|K_{23}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{|n|m^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)}{2(|n-m|+|n|)}\Big|\lesssim{\left<m\right>}^{3},

\displaystyle|K_{24}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{n^{2}m^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)+\frac{1}{2}|n|m^{4}}{{(|n-m|+|n|)}^{2}}\Big|\lesssim{\left<m\right>}^{3}.

“
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” Assume that
\phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
satisfying
H\phi=0
. Define
f=Uv\phi
, next we prove that
f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Since
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
(i)
yields that
f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and

\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2},

where
c_{1},c_{2}
are as defined in (
6.1
).
Noting that
QT_{0}f=c_{1}v^{\prime}
by
(Q-S_{0})\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=
Span
\{v^{\prime}\}
, then it suffices to show that
\left<f,v_{2}\right>=0
and
c_{1}=0
. For the former, it follows by the similar method used in “
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” of
(i)
. For the latter, observe that
G_{0}vf\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
(from “
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” direction above). Thus,

c_{1}n=\phi(n)+(G_{0}vf)(n)-c_{2}\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

which implies that
c_{1}=0
.

(iii)
“
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” For any
f\in S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, part
(ii)
indicates that there exists
\phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
with the following form:

\phi=-G_{0}vf+\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}},\ {\rm such\ that\ }H\phi=0\ {\rm and}\ f=Uv\phi.

Moreover, since
T_{0}f=0
, then
\phi=-G_{0}vf
. Therefore, it remains to prove that
G_{0}vf\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. In fact, using the orthogonality conditions
\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0
for
k=0,1,2,3
, we express

\displaystyle 12(G_{0}vf)(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|-|n|^{3}+|n|+3|n|nm-3|n|m^{2}+\frac{nm^{3}-nm}{|n|}\right)v(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}K_{3}(n,m)v(m)f(m).

(6.13)

For
n\neq 0
, we decompose
K_{3}(n,m)
as five terms:

\displaystyle K_{3}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\Big((m^{2}-1)(|n-m|-|n|)+\frac{nm(m^{2}-1)}{|n|}\Big)+\Big(\frac{nm^{3}}{|n|}-\frac{2nm^{3}}{|n-m|+|n|}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{5}{2}\Big(\frac{|n|m^{2}(|n|-|n-m|)}{|n-m|+|n|}-\frac{nm^{3}}{2|n|}\Big)+\Big(\frac{n^{2}m^{2}(|n-m|-|n|)}{{(|n-m|+|n|)}^{2}}+\frac{nm^{3}}{4|n|}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{|n|m^{4}}{2{(|n-m|+|n|)}^{2}}:=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{5}K_{3j}(n,m).

(6.14)

Clearly,
|K_{35}(n,m)|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|}
, next we show that

|K_{3j}(n,m)|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|},\quad j=1,2,3,4,

then combining (
6
), (
6
) with the assumption of
V
, we prove that
G_{0}vf\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, finding the common denominator and triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle|K_{31}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{(m^{2}-1)m^{2}|n|+nm(m^{2}-1)(|n-m|-|n|)}{(|n-m|+|n|)|n|}\Big|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|},

\displaystyle|K_{32}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{nm^{3}(|n-m|-|n|)}{(|n-m|+|n|)|n|}\Big|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|},

\displaystyle|K_{33}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{2}\Big|\frac{m^{3}n|n|(|n|-|n-m|)-\frac{1}{2}|n|m^{5}}{{(|n-m|+|n|)}^{2}|n|}\Big|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|},

\displaystyle|K_{34}(n,m)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{n^{3}m^{3}(|n-m|-|n|)-\frac{1}{4}nm^{4}(2n-m)(|n|+|n-m|)+\frac{n|n|m^{5}}{2}}{{(|n-m|+|n|)}^{3}|n|}\Big|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{4}}{|n|}.

“
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” Suppose that
\phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and satisfies
H\phi=0
. Let
f=Uv\phi
. Then it follows from
(ii)
that
f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and

\phi=-G_{0}vf+\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

Thus, it suffices to show that
\left<f,v_{3}\right>=0
and
PT_{0}f=0
. For the former, one can verify it by using the similar method in “
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” of
(i)
. For the latter, noticing that
G_{0}vf\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
from “
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” above, then

\frac{\left<T_{0}f,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}=\phi+G_{0}vf\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

which implies that
PT_{0}f=0
.

(iv)
“
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” Assume that
f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then
f\in\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0
. Recall that
\widetilde{P}=\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\cdot,\tilde{v}\right>\tilde{v}
and thus
\widetilde{Q}=I-\widetilde{P}
. Then

Uf=-\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+\widetilde{P}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=-\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\right>\tilde{v}:=-\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+c\tilde{v}.

(6.15)

Multiplying
U
from both sides of (
6.15
), one obtains that

f=-U\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+cU\tilde{v}=Uv(-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+Jc):=Uv\phi.

Firstly, we prove that
\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+Jc\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. It is enough to show that
\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Since

\displaystyle 32\sqrt{2}(\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f)(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(2\sqrt{2}|n-m|-\left(2\sqrt{2}-3\right)^{|n-m|}\Big)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(2\sqrt{2}(|n-m|-|n|)-\left(2\sqrt{2}-3\right)^{|n-m|}\Big)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

where we used the fact that
\left<f,\tilde{v}\right>
=0 in the second equality. Since
0<3-2\sqrt{2}<1
and by the triangle equality, we have

\displaystyle\left|(\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f)(n)\right|\lesssim\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|m|)|\tilde{v}(m)f(m)|\lesssim 1\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}).

Hence,
\phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, note that
(\Delta^{2}-16)J\widetilde{G}_{0}Jvf=vf,(\Delta^{2}-16)(Jc)=0
and
vf=V\phi
, then

(H-16)\phi=(\Delta^{2}-16+V)\phi=(\Delta^{2}-16)(-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+Jc)+V\phi=-vf+vf=0.

“
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” Given that
\phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
and satisfies
H\phi=16\phi
. Let
f=Uv\phi
, then
f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi(n)=-(J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f)(n)+(-1)^{n}c
with
c
defined in (
6.2
). Indeed, let
\eta
be as in
(i)
. For any
\delta>0
, define

\widetilde{F}(\delta)=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(JV\phi)(n)\eta(\delta n).

Noting that
V(n)\phi(n)=-[(\Delta^{2}-16)\phi](n)
and
J\Delta J=-\Delta-4
, we can apply the same method as in part
(i)
to obtain that

\left<f,\tilde{v}\right>=\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{F}(\delta)=-\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(J\phi)(n)[(\Delta^{2}+8\Delta)(\eta(\delta\cdot))](n)=0.

Finally, it is key to show that
\phi(n)=-(J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f)(n)+(-1)^{n}c
. Once this is established, then

\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=\widetilde{Q}(U+\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v})f=\widetilde{Q}v\phi+\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f=\widetilde{Q}v\phi+\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}(-J\phi+c)=0.

Therefore,
f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and (iv) is proved. To see this, let

\tilde{\phi}=\phi+J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f
. By a similar argument as in
(i)
, we have
\tilde{\phi}\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
(\Delta^{2}-16)\tilde{\phi}=0
, which is equivalent to
(\Delta^{2}+8\Delta)J\tilde{\phi}=0
. This implies that
J\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{c}
for some constant
\tilde{c}
. Moreover, using the condition
H\phi=16\phi
and following the similar method as in
(i)
, one can obtain that
\tilde{c}\tilde{v}=\widetilde{T}_{0}f
. Thus,
\tilde{c}=\frac{\left<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}
.

(v)
“
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” Given any
f\in\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, then
\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0
and by (iv), there exists
\phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
of the form:

\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f,\ {\rm such\ that\ }H\phi=16\phi\ {\rm and}\ f=Uv\phi.

Thus, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and the desired conclusion is obtained. To see this, we have

\displaystyle 32\sqrt{2}\big(\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\big)(n)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|n-m|\tilde{v}(m)f(m)-\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{(2\sqrt{2}-3)}^{|n-m|}\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=2\sqrt{2}F_{1}(n)-F_{2}(n).

(6.16)

For
n\neq 0
, in view that
\big<f,\tilde{v}_{k}\big>=0
for
k=0,1
, we further express
F_{1}(n)
and
F_{2}(n)
as follows:

\displaystyle F_{1}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|n-m|\tilde{v}(m)f(m)=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(|n-m|-|n|+\frac{nm}{|n|}\Big)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

(6.17)

and

\displaystyle F_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{(-q)}^{|n-m|}\tilde{v}(m)f(m)=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\big({(-q)}^{|n-m|}-{(-q)}^{|n|}\big)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{(-1)}^{|n-m|}\big({q}^{|n-m|}-q^{|n|}\big)\tilde{v}(m)f(m)+\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\big({(-1)}^{|n-m|}-(-1)^{|n|}\big)q^{|n|}\tilde{v}(m)f(m)

\displaystyle:=F_{21}(n)+F_{22}(n),

(6.18)

where
q=3-2\sqrt{2}\in(0,1)
.
Obviously,
F_{22}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, combining (
6
)
\sim
(
6
), in what follows, it suffices to prove that
F_{1},F_{21}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, which can be derived by establishing that

|\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)|+\big|{q}^{|n-m|}-q^{|n|}\big|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{2}}{|n|}.

(6.19)

Indeed,
on one hand
,

|\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)|=\left|\frac{nm(|n-m|-|n|)}{(|n-m|+|n|)|n|}+\frac{m^{2}}{|n-m|+|n|}\right|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{2}}{|n|}.

(6.20)

On the other hand
, denote
a={\rm ln}q<0
, when
|n-m|\neq|n|
, then we have

\displaystyle{q}^{|n-m|}-q^{|n|}=\frac{e^{a|n-m|}-e^{a|n|}}{|n-m|-|n|}(|n-m|-|n|):=\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)(|n-m|-|n|).

If
|n-m|>|n|
, by differential mean value theorem, one has

\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)=ae^{a|n|}e^{a\theta_{1}(|n-m|-|n|)},\quad\theta_{1}\in[0,1],

which implies that for
k=0,1
,

|n|^{k}|\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m,\theta_{1}.

Since
|n-m|-|n|=\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)-\frac{nm}{|n|}
, then

\left|{q}^{|n-m|}-q^{|n|}\right|=\left|\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)\Big(\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)-\frac{nm}{|n|}\Big)\right|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{2}}{|n|}.

(6.21)

If
|n-m|<|n|
, then

\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)=ae^{a|n-m|}e^{a\theta_{2}(|n|-|n-m|)},\quad\theta_{2}\in[0,1].

Noticing that for
k=0,1
,

|n-m|^{k}|\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m,\theta_{2},

and
|n-m|-|n|=\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)-\frac{m^{2}}{|n|}-\frac{(n-m)m}{|n|}
, we obtain that

\left|{q}^{|n-m|}-q^{|n|}\right|=\left|\widetilde{K}_{2}(n,m)\Big(\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)-\frac{m^{2}}{|n|}-\frac{(n-m)m}{|n|}\Big)\right|\lesssim\frac{{\left<m\right>}^{2}}{|n|}.

(6.22)

Therefore, combining (
6.20
)
\sim
(
6.22
), (
6.19
) is established and this proves that
\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

“
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” Supposed that
\phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
H\phi=16\phi
. Let
f=Uv\phi
, next we show that
f\in\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. First, it follows from (iv) that

f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and

\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+J\frac{\big<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\big>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

In what follows, we prove that
\big<f,\tilde{v}_{1}\big>=0
and
\widetilde{P}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0
, then the desired result is established. The former can be obtained by using the similar method in “
\bm{\Longleftarrow}
” of
(iv)
. For the latter, noticing that
J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
from “
\bm{\Longrightarrow}
” above, then

J\frac{\left<\widetilde{T}_{0}f,\tilde{v}\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}=\phi+J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

which implies that
\widetilde{P}\widetilde{T}_{0}f=0
.
∎

Appendix A
Commutator estimates and Mourre Theory

This appendix is divided into two parts. First, we review the main results of
[
30
]
, which focus on commutator estimates for a self-adjoint operator with respect to a suitable conjugate operator. These estimates establish the smoothness of the resolvent as a function of the energy between suitable spaces. Second, we collect some sufficient conditions related to the regularity of bounded self-adjoint operators with respect to conjugate operators.

For this purpose, we first introduce some notations and definitions. Let
(X,\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>)
denote a separable complex Hilbert space and
T
be a self-adjoint operator defined on
X
with domain
\mathcal{D}(T)
.

•

Define

X_{+2}:=\left(\mathcal{D}(T),\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{+2}\right),\quad\left<\varphi,\phi\right>_{+2}:=\left<\varphi,\phi\right>+\left<T\varphi,T\phi\right>,\quad\forall\ \varphi,\phi\in\mathcal{D}(T),

and let
X_{-2}
be the dual space of
X_{+2}
.

•

Let
A
be a self-adjoint operator on
X
. The sesquilinear form
[T,A]
on
\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(A)
is

defined as

[T,A](\varphi,\phi):=\left<(TA-AT)\varphi,\phi\right>,\quad\forall\ \varphi,\phi\in\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(A).

(A.1)

Definition A.1
.

Let
T
be as above and
n\geq 1
be an integer. A self-adjoint operator
A
on
X
is said to be conjugate to
T
at the point
E\in\mathbb{R}
and
T
is said to be
n
-smooth with respect to
A
, if the following conditions (a)
\sim
(e) are satisfied:

(a)

\mathcal{D}(A)\cap\mathcal{D}(T)
is a core for
T
.

(b)

e^{i\theta A}
maps
\mathcal{D}(T)
into
\mathcal{D}(T)
and for each
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(T)
,

\sup_{|\theta|\leq 1}\|Te^{i\theta A}\phi\|<\infty.

(
c_{n}
)

The form
i[T,A]
defined on
\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(A)
is bounded from below and closable. The self-adjoint operator associated with its closure is denoted by
iB_{1}
. Assume
\mathcal{D}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(B_{1})
. If
n>1
, assume for
j=2,\cdots,n
that the form
i[iB_{j-1},A]
, defined on
\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(A)
, is bounded from below and closable. The associated self-adjoint operator is denoted by
iB_{j}
, and it is assumed that
\mathcal{D}(T)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(B_{j})
.

(
d_{n}
)

The form
[B_{n},A]
, defined on
\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(A)
, extends to a bounded operator from
X_{+2}
to
X_{-2}
.

(e)

There exist
\alpha>0,\delta>0
and a compact operator
K
on
X
such that

E_{T}(\mathcal{J})iB_{1}E_{T}(\mathcal{J})\geq\alpha E_{T}(\mathcal{J})+E_{T}(\mathcal{J})KE_{T}(\mathcal{J}),

(A.2)

where
\mathcal{J}=(E-\delta,E+\delta)
is called the interval of conjugacy.

Theorem A.2
.

(
[
30
, Theorem 2.2]
)
Let
T
be as above and
n\geq 1
an integer. Let
A
be a conjugate operator to
T
at
E\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
T
is
n
-smooth with respect to
A
. Let
\mathcal{J}
be the interval of conjugacy and
I\subseteq\mathcal{J}\cap\sigma_{c}(T)

(
\sigma_{c}(T)
denotes the continuous spectrum of
T
)
a relatively compact interval. Let
s>n-\frac{1}{2}
.

(i)

For
{\Re}z\in I,{\Im}z\neq 0
, one has

\|\left<A\right>^{-s}(T-z)^{-n}\left<A\right>^{-s}\|\leq c.

(ii)

For
{\Re}z,{\Re}z^{\prime}\in I,\ 0<|{\Im}z|\leq 1,\ 0<|{\Im}z^{\prime}|\leq 1
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
z,z^{\prime}
, such that

\|\left<A\right>^{-s}((T-z)^{-n}-(T-z^{\prime})^{-n})\left<A\right>^{-s}\|\leq C|z-z^{\prime}|^{\delta_{1}},

where

\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(s,n)=\frac{1}{1+\frac{sn}{s-n+\frac{1}{2}}}.

(A.3)

(iii)

Let
\lambda\in I
. The norm limits

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left<A\right>^{-s}(T-\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)^{-n}\left<A\right>^{-s}

exist and equal

\left(\frac{d}{d\lambda}\right)^{n-1}(\left<A\right>^{-s}(T-\lambda\pm i0)^{-1}\left<A\right>^{-s}),

where

\left<A\right>^{-s}(T-\lambda\pm i0)^{-1}\left<A\right>^{-s}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left<A\right>^{-s}(T-\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)^{-1}\left<A\right>^{-s}.

The norm limits are Hölder continuous with exponent
\delta_{1}(s,n)
given above.

In verifying the conditions of this theorem, particularly conditions
(c_{n})
and
(d_{n})
, it is often more convenient to examine the regularity of
T
with respect to a suitable conjugate operator. To this end, we will revisit this concept for the case where
T
is a bounded self-adjoint operator and present some sufficient conditions to judge this regularity.

Let
T
be a bounded operator. For each integer
k
, we denote
ad^{k}_{A}(T)
as the sesquilinear form on
\mathcal{D}(A^{k})
defined iteratively as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}ad^{0}_{A}(T)&=T,\\
ad^{1}_{A}(T)&=[T,A]=TA-AT,\\
ad^{k}_{A}(T)&=ad^{1}_{A}\left(ad^{k-1}_{A}(T)\right)=\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 0,i+j=k}\frac{k!}{i!j!}(-1)^{i}A^{i}TA^{j}.\end{split}

(A.4)

Definition A.3
.

Given an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we say that
T
is of
C^{k}(A)
, denoted by
T\in C^{k}(A)
, if the sesquilinear form
ad^{k}_{A}(T)
admits a continuous extension to
X
. We identify this extension with its associated bounded operator in
X
and denote it by the same symbol.

Remark A.4
.

This property is often referred to as the regularity of
T
with respect to
A
in many contexts. Specifically,
T\in C^{k}(A)
holds if and only if the vector-valued function
f(t)\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
has the usual
C^{k}(\mathbb{R})
regularity for every
\phi\in X
, where
f
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle f:~~\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(X),\ t\longmapsto f(t)=e^{itA}Te^{itA}.

Moreover, this property satisfies the following algebraic structure.

Lemma A.5
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, let
T_{1},T_{2}
be bounded self-adjoint operators on
X
such that
T_{1},T_{2}\in C^{k}(A)
. Then,
T_{1}+T_{2}\in C^{k}(A)
and
ad^{k}_{A}(T_{1}+T_{2})=ad^{k}_{A}(T_{1})+ad^{k}_{A}(T_{2})
.

Proof.

The result follows from the case
k=1
established in
[
18
, Section 2]
, combined with an inductive argument.
∎

As an application, in particular, we consider
X=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
T=H=\Delta^{2}+V
, where
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
, and let
A
be defined as in (
2.14
). We then establish the following regularity property of
H
with respect to
A
.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
, where
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
with
\beta>1
and let
A
be defined as in (
2.14
). Then,
H\in C^{[\beta]}(A)
, where
[\beta]
denotes the biggest integral no more than
\beta
.

Proof.

First, we note that
[
5
, Lemma 4.1]
establishes that
ad^{1}_{iA}(-\Delta)=-\Delta(4+\Delta)
. Based on this, we claim that
\Delta^{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\infty}(A)
. To verify this, a direct calculation yields

ad^{1}_{iA}(\Delta^{2})=(-\Delta)[ad^{1}_{iA}(-\Delta)]+[ad^{1}_{iA}(-\Delta)](-\Delta)=2\Delta^{2}(4+\Delta).

Thus,
\Delta^{2}\in C^{1}(A)
. Repeating a similar decomposition process, one can find that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
ad^{k}_{iA}(\Delta^{2})
is a polynomial about
-\Delta
of degree
2+k
. Consequently,
\Delta^{2}\in C^{k}(A)
for all
k
, i.e.,
\Delta^{2}\in C^{\infty}(A)
.

As for the potential
V
,
[
5
, Proposition 5.1]
proves that
V\in C^{k}(A)
for some positive integer
k
if
V(n)
satisfies the following decay condition:

V(n)\rightarrow 0\ {\rm and}\ |(\mathcal{P}^{k}V)(n)|=O(|n|^{-k}),\quad|n|\rightarrow\infty.

Therefore,
V\in C^{[\beta]}(A)
under our assumption on
V
. Combining this with
\Delta^{2}\in C^{\infty}(A)
and Lemma
A.5
, we finish the proof.
∎
